⚠️ Note also that between the two MacIver quotations the chapter speaks in its own voice, recording that **fascism created a peculiar authoritarian system which strived to engender mass enthusiasm for its regime and policies** and that **in the event, Mussolini emerged as an extremely popular leader enjoying immense authority in the state**. ⚠️ Because that sentence is the chapter's own and not MacIver's, attribute it to the chapter; it makes the point that the leader principle operated through consent-production as well as coercion. ⚠️ That is where Topic 04's Gramsci becomes directly relevant, and the chapter itself makes the connection at Lesson 13.

Revision notes:
- Fascism **arose as a theory of reaction to democracy, socialism and communism**, a tendency **in the reverse direction, in support of the former outmoded, repressive, social and political conditions**. ⚠️ Reaction is **restorative** — hence distinct from conservatism (Lesson 23).
- **Dunner, *Dictionary of Political Science* (1965)**: fascism **rejects equality and substitutes the principle of hierarchy culminating in a supreme leader or dictator whose will is law**; **repudiates individualism** and holds that **all values derive from the state, against which the individual possesses no rights**; **true liberty … is found only in subjection to state authority**; the state **requires complete conformity, rigid discipline, and unquestioning obedience**; **force is legitimate which conduces to these ends**.
- ⚠️ **True liberty through subjection** is Hegel's structure with the justification removed — an analytical inference, not the chapter's own statement.
- **As against the liberal-democratic cult of reason, fascism relies on faith and emotion as the motive force of human actions.**
- **Instead of regarding individual as an end and the state as a means, fascism exalts the state as an end and reduces individual to the means.** ⚠️ The exact inversion of Topic 03's classical-liberal sentence.
- **Monopoly of the nation-state in all internal and external matters**; no association may **compete with the state for the loyalty of individual**; thus it **rejects the pluralistic cult of liberal-democracy**; internationally it **relies upon military solutions**.
- **It seeks unity through homogeneity**; in Germany, **racist doctrines and the programme of liquidation of 'non-Aryan' elements**. ⚠️ Equality regulates difference; homogeneity eliminates it.
- MacIver on Mussolini: **contempt for democracy**; the **'decaying corpse of the Goddess of Liberty'**; tearing down the parliamentary structure **piece by piece**; **all political parties except 'the' party** abolished; premier → **'head of the government'**; **the party the organ of the state** from **the local party boss to the Grand Council of Fascism**; **boundless obedience**; the **blackshirt Militia**; the structure **built inside the pre-existing system** until the old order was **a hollow facade** — **the king still 'reigned', the senate still met**.
- Summary: **fascism set aside constitutional democracy in order to enhance the prestige and power of the dictator and to establish the hegemony of a single party**.

### Concept check

**Question:** "True liberty is found only in subjection to state authority." How should an answer handle this proposition — and what does it reveal about the chapter's claim that fascism distorts Idealism?

**Model answer:** It should be decoded before it is attacked, because its form is not self-evidently absurd. The proposition appears in the chapter's quotation from Dunner's *Dictionary of Political Science* (1965), alongside the claims that fascism **repudiates individualism** and holds that **all values derive from the state, against which the individual possesses no rights** (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.66). Its structure — freedom achieved **through** submission rather than against it — is not invented by fascism: the chapter's own Idealism box records that **Hegel called for absolute submission of man to the state for the realization of his freedom** (PDF p.65). ⚠️ What distinguishes the idealist original is that it supplies an **account of why** the state could be the vehicle of freedom, namely that social institutions are **the expression of development of idea or consciousness which culminates in the emergence of nation-state**. Remove that account and the sentence retains its grammar while losing its justification: submission remains, but to a will rather than to a realised rational order, and the Dunner passage makes that will explicit — **a supreme leader or dictator whose will is law**. ⚠️ This is the most precise statement available of what the chapter's phrase **only to its distorted form** amounts to, and because the chapter does not itself spell it out, an answer must offer it as an **analytical inference** rather than as the source's claim. ⚠️ The attack then writes itself: the Dunner passage also concedes that the individual **possesses no rights** against the state, so the sentence cannot mean liberty in any sense that includes protection from the authority one is subject to — it has redefined the word rather than located the thing.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat fascism's rejection of equality as a rejection of unity or of community. The chapter is explicit that it **seeks unity through homogeneity** — unity is wanted intensely, but reached by sameness rather than by equal standing among people who differ. That is why the German application appears as a **programme of liquidation of 'non-Aryan' elements**, and why "fascism dislikes solidarity" would be exactly the wrong thing to write.

## Lesson 11 — Fascism as a theory of counter-revolution: Laski and the capitalism-democracy dilemma

Progress: 11 / 24 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: How a revolutionary movement became counter-revolutionary, and whose interests the chapter says it served

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§4, 6 and 8 with the Advanced companion's §3 step 7 and §5 on Laski, read against the source chapter's section headed "Theory of Counter-revolution" (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF pp.67-68), which supplies the transformation claim, the two concentration/diffusion moves, the price-of-support argument, the four named economic consequences, Laski's two passages and the chapter's institutional catalogue.
CA search: Not re-run for this lesson; see Lesson 1.
CA found: As reported there.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — the argument, as a two-phase mechanism**

```text
   PHASE 1 -- THE TRANSFORMATION                     (PDF p.67)
   "Fascism BEGAN as a movement demanding REVOLUTIONARY CHANGES
    in the social and political structure of Italy. But it soon
    developed into a FORCE AGAINST REVOLUTION ITSELF, especially
    against the revolutionary force of COMMUNISM."
                              |
   PHASE 2 -- WHY IT IS 'COUNTER-REVOLUTIONARY'
   it sought BOTH:
      (i) "to promote CONCENTRATION OF ECONOMIC CONTROL IN FEWER
           HANDS"
     (ii) "to STOP DIFFUSION OF POLITICAL POWER"
                              |
   = "it STRIPPED CAPITALISM OF ITS DEMOCRATIC CHARACTER which
      could have had some SOBERING EFFECT on it"
                              |
   THE PRICE-OF-SUPPORT ARGUMENT
   LIBERAL phase of capitalism: capitalists are "somewhat
   prepared to PAY THE PRICE of people's support in the form of
   some CONCESSIONS"
   FASCIST phase: "the capitalists are NO LONGER REQUIRED TO PAY
   THAT PRICE"; the profit motive "is no longer restrained by
   people's demands or expectations"
                              |
   FOUR NAMED RESULTS
   "LOWER WAGES for the workers | INFERIOR GENERAL CONDITIONS OF
    INDUSTRY | LOWERING OF TAXATION UPON CAPITAL | CONTRACTION OF
    SOCIAL SERVICES"
                              |
   LASKI'S FORMULATION (State in Theory and Practice; 1935)
   "FASCISM CAME TO RESCUE CAPITALISM FROM THIS DILEMMA."
```

Caption: the argument's power is that it explains a *change* — from revolutionary movement to counter-revolutionary force — by a mechanism rather than by inconsistency.

### 1. The transformation, and why it needs explaining

**Fascism began as a movement demanding revolutionary changes in the social and political structure of Italy. But it soon developed into a force against revolution itself, especially against the revolutionary force of communism** (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.67).

⚠️ **Lesson 8 set this up deliberately** by recording that the *fasci* were **of workers** and were **organized to bring about revolutionary changes**. The chapter is therefore not accusing fascism of hypocrisy; it is describing a **trajectory** and then explaining it. ⚠️ An answer that notices the set-up — origin as revolutionary, development as counter-revolutionary — is tracking the chapter's own structure.

### 2. The two defining moves

The chapter's criterion for calling fascism counter-revolutionary is precise and dual: it **sought to promote concentration of economic control in fewer hands as also to stop diffusion of political power**.

⚠️ **Both halves are necessary and they work in opposite directions on different quantities.** Economic control is **concentrated**; political power's **diffusion** is **stopped**. ⚠️ A regime that concentrated economic control while diffusing political power would be ordinary oligarchic capitalism under democracy; a regime that stopped political diffusion without concentrating economic control would be ordinary authoritarianism. Fascism, on this account, is the conjunction — and it is the conjunction that makes it counter-revolutionary rather than merely reactionary or merely authoritarian.

The consequence is stated in a phrase worth memorising: **it stripped capitalism of its democratic character which could have had some sobering effect on it**.

⚠️ **"Sobering effect" is doing precise work.** Democracy is being credited not with transforming capitalism but with **moderating** it — the same role Topic 03 assigned to the welfare state as a **rectifying mechanism** and Lesson 7 assigned to evolutionary socialism as **an effective instrument for mitigating the rigours of capitalism**. ⚠️ Fascism removes the moderator. That is the cleanest statement of its economic function available in the chapter.

### 3. The price-of-support argument

> While the masses in a capitalist society could use their political power, thanks to the development of democracy, to secure increasing material well-being, fascism ruled out such possibility. Under fascism, the masses are left with no rights or safeguards against their oppression by the ruling clique. In the liberal phase of capitalism, the capitalists are somewhat prepared to pay the price of people's support in the form of some concessions, but when it enters the fascist phase, the capitalists are no longer required to pay that price.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.68 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **This is a transactional model of democratic capitalism and it is the key to the whole lesson.** Concessions are **the price of people's support**; democracy is the mechanism that makes the price collectible; fascism abolishes the mechanism and therefore the price. ⚠️ Note the hedge **somewhat prepared** — the chapter does not claim capitalists welcome concessions, only that under democracy they must pay them.

The chapter then names four consequences: **lower wages for the workers, inferior general conditions of industry, lowering of taxation upon capital and contraction of social services**.

⚠️ **Reproduce all four.** They cover wages, working conditions, fiscal incidence and public provision — four distinct channels, not one restated. ⚠️ Note particularly **lowering of taxation upon capital**, which is the fiscal signature of the arrangement and the one most often dropped.

### 4. Laski's two passages

The chapter introduces him precisely: **Harold J. Laski, in his State in Theory and Practice (1935), observes that so long as the marriage of capitalism and democracy continued, capitalism continued to extend concessions to the masses, giving them a sense of satisfaction. But when it sought to withdraw those concessions, it had recourse to fascism.**

> Fascism came to rescue capitalism from this dilemma. By the abrogation of democracy, in one form or another, it has entrusted unlimited political power to those who own and control the means of production … All political parties which deny its purposes have been suppressed. The free trade unions have gone, and, with them, the right to strike. Wages have been reduced either unilaterally by the employers, or with the approval of the state. The right to free criticism has been suppressed; and the power of the electorate to change its government has been withdrawn.
>
> — Harold J. Laski, *State in Theory and Practice* (1935), as quoted by Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.68

⚠️ **The passage names five withdrawals, and listing them is what gives an answer its specificity**: political parties **suppressed**; **free trade unions** gone and with them **the right to strike**; wages **reduced either unilaterally by the employers, or with the approval of the state**; **the right to free criticism** suppressed; and **the power of the electorate to change its government** withdrawn.

⚠️ **The phrase "or with the approval of the state" is the most telling detail in the list.** It shows the state is not neutral between employer and worker but an active party to the reduction — which is the institutional form of the whole counter-revolution thesis.

The second Laski passage goes to the doctrine itself:

> Mussolini has explained that the individual fulfils himself in the fulfilment of the state-end… Once we examine the actual character of that end in fascist communities, it becomes clear that it implies the sacrifice of the ordinary worker to the capitalist need to make profit.
>
> — Harold J. Laski, as quoted by Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.68, marked **(ibid.)**

⚠️ **Laski's method here is worth naming because it is a model for answer-writing.** He takes fascism's own self-description — the individual **fulfils himself in the fulfilment of the state-end** — and then asks what **the actual character of that end** is in practice. ⚠️ The argument is not that the doctrine is false but that its content, once filled in by the regime's conduct, turns out to be **the sacrifice of the ordinary worker to the capitalist need to make profit**. That is an internal critique, and internal critiques are harder to answer than external ones.

⚠️ **Scan-artefact disclosure.** The extraction prints Laski's work once as **"The State in Theory' and Practice"** with a stray apostrophe; rendered *State in Theory and Practice* and catalogued.

### 5. The institutional catalogue

The chapter supplies its own list of what fascist states did to information and institutions: **fascist states also sought to curb individual liberty by obstructing the supply of true news. They brought the press, the wireless, the publishing trade, the cinema and the theatre directly under government control. The neutrality of the civil service was openly abandoned, and even the judiciary was subordinated to the service of fascist ideals. The fascist states built their authority by relying on the loyalty of the armed forces as also by arming the forces of their own partisans. The whole state apparatus was used to advance the interests of capitalism at the expense of the worker.**

⚠️ **Three groups of institutions are named and they should be kept distinct**: **information** — press, wireless, publishing trade, cinema, theatre; **impartial institutions** — the **neutrality of the civil service** and the **judiciary**; and **force** — the **armed forces** plus **the forces of their own partisans**. ⚠️ The third is the significant one: a regime that arms **its own partisans** alongside the regular armed forces has created a parallel instrument of coercion answerable to the party rather than the state, which is MacIver's **blackshirt Militia** from Lesson 10 described functionally.

### 6. The chapter's summary, and the honest limit

**In a nutshell, fascism seeks to avert the transformation of the capitalist system into a democratic welfare state or service-state. It tends to maintain the exploitative character of the capitalist patterns of production in the name of national interest, national unity, discipline, industrial peace and higher production, and to save a decadent capitalist system from the revolutionary threat of the oppressed classes.**

⚠️ **Note the five legitimating slogans the chapter names** — **national interest, national unity, discipline, industrial peace and higher production** — and note that each is independently attractive. That is precisely the point: the counter-revolutionary function is performed under descriptions no one would object to in isolation.

❌ **The honest limit, which an answer must state under a "critically examine" directive.** This is a **functional** argument: it identifies whose interests the arrangement served, and the canonical owner itself warns that **linking these features into one economic causal claim would require further argument**. ⚠️ Showing that fascism **benefited** concentrated capital does not by itself show that capital **produced** it — and the chapter supplies the counterweight itself at Lesson 13, where Ebenstein denies that the Marxist identification of fascism with capitalism **is borne out by the facts** and locates the cause in **weak democracy** plus depression-bred fear. ⚠️ A candidate who states the Laski thesis at full strength **and** flags the functional/causal gap is doing the work the directive asks for.

Revision notes:
- Fascism **began as a movement demanding revolutionary changes** but **soon developed into a force against revolution itself, especially against the revolutionary force of communism**.
- The dual criterion: **concentration of economic control in fewer hands** **and** stopping **diffusion of political power**. ⚠️ The **conjunction** is what makes it counter-revolutionary.
- It **stripped capitalism of its democratic character which could have had some sobering effect on it**.
- Price-of-support: in the **liberal phase** capitalists are **somewhat prepared to pay the price of people's support in the form of some concessions**; in the **fascist phase** they are **no longer required to pay that price**; the profit motive is **no longer restrained by people's demands or expectations**.
- Four results: **lower wages for the workers, inferior general conditions of industry, lowering of taxation upon capital and contraction of social services**.
- **Laski, *State in Theory and Practice* (1935)**: so long as **the marriage of capitalism and democracy** continued, concessions flowed; when capitalism **sought to withdraw those concessions, it had recourse to fascism**. **Fascism came to rescue capitalism from this dilemma**, entrusting **unlimited political power to those who own and control the means of production**; five withdrawals — parties **suppressed**; **free trade unions** and **the right to strike** gone; wages **reduced either unilaterally by the employers, or with the approval of the state**; **the right to free criticism** suppressed; **the power of the electorate to change its government** withdrawn.
- Laski's internal critique: Mussolini says the individual **fulfils himself in the fulfilment of the state-end**; examine **the actual character of that end** and it implies **the sacrifice of the ordinary worker to the capitalist need to make profit**.
- Institutions: information — **the press, the wireless, the publishing trade, the cinema and the theatre**; impartiality — **the neutrality of the civil service** abandoned, **the judiciary … subordinated**; force — **the armed forces** plus **arming the forces of their own partisans**.
- Summary: fascism **seeks to avert the transformation of the capitalist system into a democratic welfare state or service-state**, under the slogans of **national interest, national unity, discipline, industrial peace and higher production**.
- ❌ The honest limit: this is a **functional** argument; benefit does not establish causation, and Ebenstein (Lesson 13) denies the causal version.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the chapter need **both** limbs — concentrating economic control and stopping the diffusion of political power — to call fascism counter-revolutionary?

**Model answer:** Because either limb alone describes something else. The chapter's criterion is that fascism **sought to promote concentration of economic control in fewer hands as also to stop diffusion of political power** (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.67). A regime that concentrated economic control while leaving political power diffusing would be ordinary capitalism operating under a working democracy — which is precisely the arrangement the chapter describes as the **liberal phase**, where capitalists are **somewhat prepared to pay the price of people's support in the form of some concessions**. A regime that merely stopped political diffusion without concentrating economic control would be a familiar authoritarianism, repressive but not specifically **counter-revolutionary** in the sense of reversing a social trend. ⚠️ It is the conjunction that reverses both movements at once, and the chapter names the result exactly: fascism **stripped capitalism of its democratic character which could have had some sobering effect on it**. ⚠️ The four consequences then follow mechanically, because with the price no longer collectible there is nothing to restrain the profit motive: **lower wages for the workers, inferior general conditions of industry, lowering of taxation upon capital and contraction of social services**. ⚠️ Under a critical directive the gap must be flagged: this establishes what fascism **did** and whom it **benefited**, not that capital **caused** it, and the chapter itself supplies the counterweight at Lesson 13 where Ebenstein holds that the Marxist identification of fascism with capitalism in decay **is not borne out by the facts**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the counter-revolution thesis as a charge of hypocrisy about fascism's revolutionary origins. The chapter deliberately records at PDF p.64 that the *fasci* were **of workers** and were **organized to bring about revolutionary changes**, and then explains the shift by a mechanism. The claim is about a **trajectory with a cause**, not about insincerity — and treating it as the latter loses the argument's analytical content.

## Lesson 12 — The myth against the economic conception of history

CA found: As reported there.

🖼️ **VISUAL — three substitutions, each answering a Marxist claim**

```text
   MARXIST CLAIM                 ->  FASCIST SUBSTITUTION (p.69)
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   MATERIALISM                   ->  "a MYSTICAL POLITICAL IDEALISM"
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   CLASS CONFLICT                ->  "the ORGANIC UNITY of the
                                      NATION-STATE that claims to
                                      represent a UNIFIED NATIONAL
                                      INTEREST"
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   the ECONOMIC MOTIVE behind    ->  repudiated outright:
   human actions "which could        "fascism, now and always,
   give rise to CLASS STRUGGLE"       believes in HOLINESS and in
                                      HEROISM; that is to say, in
                                      ACTIONS INFLUENCED BY NO
                                      ECONOMIC MOTIVE, DIRECT OR
                                      INDIRECT"      (Mussolini, 1932)
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   REASON as the route to truth  ->  "it EULOGIZES THE CREATIVE
                                      POWER OF THE MYTH to muster
                                      the support of ALL CLASSES"
                                            |
                              +-------------+-------------+
                              |                           |
                    MUSSOLINI: MYTH OF THE NATION   HITLER: MYTH OF
                    "Our myth is the NATION, our     RACE, "a
                     myth is the GREATNESS OF THE    counterpart of
                     NATION." (Naples, 1922)         the fascist
                                                     image of the
                                                     nation"; the
                                                     "MIRACULOUS
                                                     POWER OF
                                                     PROPAGANDA,
                                                     especially of
                                                     FALSE PROPA-
                                                     GANDA" (Mein
                                                     Kampf, 1925-26)
```

Caption: the fourth row is the deepest. Fascism does not merely disagree with Marxism's conclusions; it rejects the **method** by which conclusions are reached.

### 1. The three substitutions

The chapter states them compactly: **fascism also seeks to dilute the revolutionary content of communism by an improvised anti-Marxist theory. It tries to replace materialism by a mystical political idealism. It rejects the theory of class-conflict in favour of the organic unity of the nation-state that claims to represent a unified national interest. It even repudiates the economic motive behind human actions which could give rise to class struggle** (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.69).

⚠️ **The word "improvised" is the chapter's own and it connects back to Lesson 8.** An **improvised** anti-Marxist theory is exactly what a doctrine assembled from **unrelated principles woven into an incoherent whole** would produce — the incoherence claim and the anti-Marxism are the same phenomenon described twice.

⚠️ **Note the escalation across the three substitutions.** The first replaces a **metaphysics**; the second replaces a **social theory**; the third repudiates a **motivational premise** — that economic interest moves people at all. ⚠️ The third is the most radical, because class conflict in Topic 04 was derived from the ownership pattern generating opposed interests. Deny that economic interest motivates, and the derivation never starts.

⚠️ **"Organic unity" and "claims to represent" should both be reproduced.** The chapter writes that the nation-state **claims to represent a unified national interest** — a hedge marking the claim as the doctrine's own assertion rather than an established fact.

### 2. Mussolini on the economic conception of history

> Fascism, now and always, believes in holiness and in heroism; that is to say, in actions influenced by no economic motive, direct or indirect. And if the economic conception of history be denied, according to which theory men are no more than puppets, carried to and fro by the waves of chance while the real directing forces are quite out of their control, it follows that the existence of an unchangeable and unchanging class-war is also denied—the natural progeny of the economic conception of history. And above all fascism denies that class-war can be the preponderant force in the transformation of society.
>
> — Benito Mussolini, in his article in *Encyclopaedia Italiana* (1932), as quoted by Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.69

⚠️ **The passage contains an argument, and reconstructing it is worth real marks.** Its structure is: fascism believes in **holiness** and **heroism**, i.e. in **actions influenced by no economic motive, direct or indirect** → if the **economic conception of history** is denied → then **the existence of an unchangeable and unchanging class-war is also denied**, since class-war is **the natural progeny of the economic conception of history** → therefore **class-war** cannot be **the preponderant force in the transformation of society**.

⚠️ **Note how Mussolini characterises the position he is attacking**: on the economic conception, **men are no more than puppets, carried to and fro by the waves of chance while the real directing forces are quite out of their control**. ⚠️ That is a caricature of historical materialism — Topic 04 showed the chapter explicitly denying that **the superstructure is wholly dependent on the base** and recognising **a sort of interdependence**. ⚠️ Saying so is the correct critical move: Mussolini's argument succeeds against the deterministic reading that Marxism itself abandoned, and fails against the position as Topic 04 actually reconstructs it. That is a genuinely strong line in an answer, because it attacks the argument's **target** rather than merely its conclusion.

⚠️ **A second critical point.** Mussolini's premise is that fascism believes in actions with **no economic motive, direct or indirect** — yet Lesson 11's whole counter-revolution analysis holds that fascism's actual function was the protection of concentrated economic interest, and Lesson 13 records Laski's conclusion that Italian fascism was **an insistence upon compulsory obedience to a state whose purpose is to protect existing class-relations**. ⚠️ The denial of economic motive is therefore precisely what the chapter's own analysis contradicts — and pointing out that tension between the doctrine's self-description and the chapter's account of its practice is exactly what a "critically examine" directive rewards.

### 3. Myth against reason: the Naples speech

**Fascism thus tends to project an image of a unified nation with an indivisible interest to repudiate the theory of class-conflict. It even refuses to rely on reason for arriving at truth. Instead, it eulogizes the creative power of the myth to muster the support of all classes in society.**

> We have created our myth. The myth is a faith, it is passion. It is not necessary that it shall be a reality. It is a reality by the fact that it is a goad, a hope, a faith, that it is courage. Our myth is the nation, our myth is the greatness of the nation.
>
> — Benito Mussolini, in a speech at Naples in 1922, as quoted by Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.69

⚠️ **This is the most important quotation in the entire fascism arc, and the sentence to dwell on is the third.** **It is not necessary that it shall be a reality.** ⚠️ That is an explicit abandonment of truth as a criterion, made by the doctrine's founder, in public. The replacement criterion is supplied in the next sentence: a myth **is a reality by the fact that it is a goad, a hope, a faith, that it is courage** — that is, by its **effects on action**, not by its correspondence to anything.

⚠️ **Three consequences worth stating.**

1. ⚠️ It explains why the chapter files fascism under **political pathology** rather than political theory. A doctrine that has replaced the truth-criterion with an efficacy-criterion cannot be assessed as a theory at all, because theories are the kind of thing that can be false.
2. ⚠️ It connects to Lesson 10's **faith and emotion as the motive force of human actions** against **the liberal-democratic cult of reason** — the Naples speech is that contrast stated by the doctrine itself.
3. ⚠️ It connects forward to Lesson 17's **Sorel**, who also **recommended the use of 'myth' to mobilize masses into action** and held that the power of the general strike **could be exaggerated to elevate it to the level of a 'myth'**. ⚠️ Two doctrines at opposite political poles reach for the same instrument, which is a striking and genuinely useful comparative observation — the myth-technique is **not** the property of any one political position.

### 4. Hitler and the myth of race

**Hitler similarly created the myth of race and developed his racist doctrine as a counterpart of the fascist image of the nation. He dwelled on the miraculous power of propaganda, especially of false propaganda, in his notorious work Mein Kampf ('My Struggle') (1925-26). Thus, fascism openly sets aside truth and reason for the advancement of its sinister designs and containment of revolutionary forces.**

⚠️ **Scan-artefact disclosure.** The extraction prints **"workMein-Kampf"** without a space and with a hyphen; rendered **work *Mein Kampf*** and catalogued.

⚠️ **"Counterpart" is the precise word and it should be preserved.** The myth of race is **a counterpart of** the fascist image of the nation — parallel in function, different in content. ⚠️ That is exactly the **chief variant** relation of Lesson 8: Nazism occupies the same structural position with different material, which is why it is neither identical to Italian fascism nor a separate doctrine.

⚠️ **Note the phrase "especially of false propaganda".** The chapter is recording that the efficacy-criterion of the Naples speech was theorised explicitly on the German side — falsity is not an accidental by-product but an acknowledged instrument.

### 5. How to use this lesson

⚠️ In an answer asking how fascism relates to Marxism, run four points in this order:

| | Point |
|---|---|
| 1 | Fascism's anti-Marxism is **improvised**, consistent with its general incoherence |
| 2 | It substitutes **mystical political idealism** for materialism and **organic unity of the nation-state** for class conflict |
| 3 | Its deepest move is to deny the **economic motive** itself, which cuts off the derivation of class conflict at its root (Mussolini, 1932) |
| 4 | But it then abandons the truth-criterion altogether (Naples, 1922), which is why the chapter treats it as pathology rather than theory — and which exposes it to the charge that its own denial of economic motive is contradicted by its economic function (Lesson 11) |

Revision notes:
- Fascism seeks to **dilute the revolutionary content of communism by an improvised anti-Marxist theory**.
- Three substitutions: **materialism** → **a mystical political idealism**; **class-conflict** → **the organic unity of the nation-state that claims to represent a unified national interest**; and repudiation of **the economic motive behind human actions which could give rise to class struggle**.
- **Mussolini, *Encyclopaedia Italiana* (1932)**: fascism **believes in holiness and in heroism; that is to say, in actions influenced by no economic motive, direct or indirect**; if **the economic conception of history be denied** — under which **men are no more than puppets, carried to and fro by the waves of chance** — then **an unchangeable and unchanging class-war is also denied**, being **the natural progeny of the economic conception of history**; **fascism denies that class-war can be the preponderant force in the transformation of society**.
- ⚠️ The puppets characterisation is a **caricature** of historical materialism; Topic 04 records the chapter itself denying that the superstructure is **wholly dependent** on the base.
- ⚠️ The denial of economic motive contradicts the chapter's own counter-revolution analysis (Lesson 11) and Laski's verdict (Lesson 13).
- **It even refuses to rely on reason for arriving at truth**; it **eulogizes the creative power of the myth to muster the support of all classes**.
- **Mussolini, Naples, 1922**: **we have created our myth. The myth is a faith, it is passion. It is not necessary that it shall be a reality. It is a reality by the fact that it is a goad, a hope, a faith, that it is courage. Our myth is the nation, our myth is the greatness of the nation.**
- ⚠️ **It is not necessary that it shall be a reality** — the truth-criterion is replaced by an **efficacy** criterion.
- **Hitler similarly created the myth of race … as a counterpart of the fascist image of the nation**; the **miraculous power of propaganda, especially of false propaganda**, in ***Mein Kampf* ('My Struggle') (1925-26)**.
- ⚠️ Sorel (Lesson 17) also uses **myth** as a mobilising instrument — the technique belongs to no single political position.

### Concept check

**Question:** Mussolini's 1932 argument denies the economic conception of history and concludes that class-war cannot be the preponderant force in social transformation. Where does the argument fail, and where does it have force?

**Model answer:** It has force against the version of historical materialism it names, and fails against the version the tradition actually defends. Mussolini characterises the economic conception as the theory under which **men are no more than puppets, carried to and fro by the waves of chance while the real directing forces are quite out of their control** (as quoted at Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.69). Against **that** — a crude determinism in which human agency is nil — the argument lands, and it is the reason the chapter can call fascism's assertion of **holiness and … heroism** rhetorically effective. ⚠️ But Topic 04 showed the source itself rejecting precisely that reading: **it is no longer believed that the superstructure is wholly dependent on the base**, with **a sort of interdependence** recognised, and Gramsci giving the superstructure **some degree of autonomy**. Historical materialism as reconstructed there claims that economic relations **play important role in shaping** social, political, intellectual and ethical relationships — a weighting claim, not a puppetry claim — and Mussolini's argument has no purchase on it. ⚠️ There is a second and sharper failure, internal to the chapter. Mussolini's premise is that fascist action is **influenced by no economic motive, direct or indirect**; yet the chapter's own counter-revolution analysis holds that fascism **sought to promote concentration of economic control in fewer hands**, producing **lower wages**, **lowering of taxation upon capital** and **contraction of social services**, and Laski concludes that Italian fascism was **an insistence upon compulsory obedience to a state whose purpose is to protect existing class-relations**. ⚠️ The denial of economic motive is therefore contradicted by the economic function the chapter documents — which is the strongest available line against the 1932 argument, because it uses the source's own evidence rather than an external premise.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the myth-technique as a distinguishing mark of fascism. Lesson 17 records that Sorel, at the opposite political pole, **recommended the use of 'myth' to mobilize masses into action** and held that the general strike's power **could be exaggerated to elevate it to the level of a 'myth'**. What distinguishes fascism in the Naples speech is not the use of myth but the explicit abandonment of the truth-criterion — **it is not necessary that it shall be a reality** — in favour of efficacy.

## Lesson 13 — Socio-economic basis, the two critiques, and the chapter's verdict

Progress: 13 / 24 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Who produced fascism, the liberal and Marxist critiques, and the refusal to equate fascism with communist totalitarianism
