🖼️ **VISUAL — the dispute about fascism's social base**

```text
   THESIS A -- "a movement of the LOWER MIDDLE CLASS"
   held by W.M. McGOVERN (From Luther to Hitler; 1941) and
   R.M. MacIVER (The Web of Government; 1965)
                              |
   THE CHAPTER'S OWN REJECTION:            (PDF pp.69-70)
   "when examined closely, this interpretation DOES NOT SEEM TO
    BE CORRECT"
   -- CONCEDED: fascism "particularly APPEALED to small
      businessmen, such as individual shopkeepers, who felt their
      livelihood threatened ... by the rising WORKING CLASS ...
      and ... by the monster of MONOPOLY CAPITALISM"
   -- BUT: "fascism itself DID NOT EMANATE FROM ANY MOVEMENT
      LAUNCHED BY the lower middle class"
   -- AND: "the fascist MILITIA were recruited from a SUBCLASS OF
      THE TRADELESS DREGS of the working class WITHOUT CLASS
      LOYALTY OR SELF-RESPECT"
                              |
   THE CHAPTER'S TEST: "to discover the real socio-economic basis
   of fascism, it is essential to determine WHICH CLASS IT SOUGHT
   TO SERVE"
                              |
   THESIS B -- LASKI: it served "the interests of the CAPITALIST
   CLASS at the expense of the masses, especially the working
   class"
   "Stripped of all its rhetorical trappings Italian fascism
    appears quite simply as an INSISTENCE UPON COMPULSORY
    OBEDIENCE TO A STATE WHOSE PURPOSE IS TO PROTECT EXISTING
    CLASS-RELATIONS"
                              |
   THESIS C -- EBENSTEIN: not capitalism but WEAK DEMOCRACY
   "the conditions of capitalism DO NOT BY THEMSELVES give rise
    to fascism, but it arises ONLY WHERE DEMOCRACY IS
    PARTICULARLY WEAK"
```

Caption: three positions, and the chapter rejects the first, endorses the second, and then records the third as a qualification it treats as compatible with the second.

### 1. The lower-middle-class thesis, conceded and rejected

The chapter names its holders — **W.M. McGovern (*From Luther to Hitler*; 1941)** and **R.M. MacIver (*The Web of Government*; 1965)** — and then rejects the thesis while conceding its evidence.

- ✅ **Conceded:** fascism **particularly appealed to small businessmen, such as individual shopkeepers, who felt their livelihood threatened on the one hand by the rising working class with its revolutionary socialism, and on the other by the monster of monopoly capitalism**.
- ✅ **Rejected:** **fascism itself did not emanate from any movement launched by the lower middle class.**
- ✅ **The counter-evidence:** **the fascist militia were recruited from a subclass of the tradeless dregs of the working class without class loyalty or self-respect.**

⚠️ **The distinction being drawn is between appeal and origin, and it is analytically precise.** A doctrine can appeal strongly to a class without having been launched by it. ⚠️ Note the squeeze described: shopkeepers threatened **on the one hand** by revolutionary socialism and **on the other** by **monopoly capitalism** — a class with enemies above and below, which is why it is receptive to a doctrine that promises order against both.

⚠️ **The militia-recruitment claim is the chapter's own and is expressed in strongly pejorative language** — **tradeless dregs**, **without class loyalty or self-respect**. ⚠️ Reproduce it as the chapter's wording rather than adopting it in one's own voice; the analytical content is that the armed instrument was drawn from people with **no class position to defend**, which is what makes them available for service to any paymaster.

The chapter then states its own method: **to discover the real socio-economic basis of fascism, it is essential to determine which class it sought to serve.**

⚠️ **This is a functional test and it should be named as such**, because it is exactly the test whose limits Lesson 11 flagged. Asking **which class it served** will reliably identify beneficiaries; whether beneficiaries are causes is a further question.

### 2. Laski's answer

**As Laski, in his State in Theory and Practice (1935) has amply demonstrated, fascism sought to serve the interests of the capitalist class at the expense of the masses, especially the working class. It created the myth of the nation to secure concentration of economic and political control in the hands of a small number of persons and to demand unquestioning obedience and devotion from the masses to the authority so created.** Then the conclusion:

> Stripped of all its rhetorical trappings Italian fascism appears quite simply as an insistence upon compulsory obedience to a state whose purpose is to protect existing class-relations.
>
> — Harold J. Laski, as quoted by Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.70

⚠️ **This is the single most quotable sentence on fascism's function, and its structure is worth noting.** **Stripped of all its rhetorical trappings** performs the same operation Laski performed at Lesson 11 — take the doctrine's self-description, remove it, and inspect what remains. ⚠️ Note that the myth of Lesson 12 is here given a **function**: it was **created … to secure concentration of economic and political control**, which is the chapter joining its own Lesson 11 and Lesson 12 material into one argument.

### 3. Ebenstein's qualification, in three passages

The chapter introduces him as having **tried to show that the conditions of capitalism do not by themselves give rise to fascism, but it arises only where democracy is particularly weak**.

> Industrialists are not, as a class, any more fascist-minded than other social groups; in countries with strong liberal and democratic traditions, for example, industrialists have neither more nor less faith than other people in the democratic process. But where democracy has been weak, as it was in Germany, Italy, and Japan, it took only a few wealthy industrialists and landowners to supply fascist movements with ample funds.
>
> — William Ebenstein, *Today's Isms* (1980), as quoted by Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.70

> In times of depression, fear and frustration undermine faith in the democratic process, and where the faith in rational methods weakens, fascism is the potential gainer. The small businessman blames big business for his troubles; big business puts the blame on the unreasonableness of the labour unions; labour feels that the only way out is to soak the rich; the farmers feel that they are not getting enough for farm products and that the prices they pay for manufactured goods are too high; and—worst of all—there is the large mass of unemployed people.
>
> — William Ebenstein, as quoted by Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.70, marked **(ibid.)**

> It is among these spiritually homeless that fascism makes serious inroads during a depression: by putting an unemployed person into a uniform, a fascist movement makes him feel that he 'belongs', and by telling him that he is a member of a superior race or nation, such a movement restores some of his self-respect.
>
> — William Ebenstein, as quoted by Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.71, marked **(ibid.)**

⚠️ **Three distinct contributions, and all three are usable.**

1. **The first passage decouples industrialists from fascism as a class** while conceding that **it took only a few wealthy industrialists and landowners to supply fascist movements with ample funds**. ⚠️ Note the elegance of the concession: funding is compatible with the class as a whole being no more fascist-minded than any other, because funding requires only **a few**.
2. **The second passage describes a structure of mutual blame** — small business blames big business, big business blames unions, labour wants to **soak the rich**, farmers blame prices, and **worst of all** there is **the large mass of unemployed people**. ⚠️ The mechanism is that depression produces not a single grievance but a circle of grievances with no agreed culprit, which is precisely the condition under which a doctrine that supplies an external enemy becomes attractive.
3. **The third passage is the most precise psychological claim in the arc.** The chapter introduces it by saying Ebenstein **further argues that even the economic suffering caused by unemployment can be mitigated by adequate relief, but the feeling of being useless, unwanted, and outside the productive ranks of society paves the way for the rise of fascism**. ⚠️ So the operative deprivation is **status**, not income — relief can address income and leave the deprivation untouched. The fascist offer is then exactly matched to it: a **uniform** supplies belonging, and membership of **a superior race or nation** supplies **self-respect**. ⚠️ This is the mechanism by which Lesson 10's **unity through homogeneity** becomes attractive to individuals, and connecting the two is a strong analytical move.

### 4. How the chapter reconciles Laski and Ebenstein

**The basic point made by Ebenstein to vindicate capitalism is, however, not fundamentally different from Laski's view. The basic point is that so long as capitalism can accommodate the democratic aspirations of the people, it is not likely to degenerate into fascism. Ebenstein is highly optimistic of this potential in capitalism. But Laski further holds that if the marriage between capitalism and democracy is brought to its logical conclusion, it will lead to the transformation of capitalism itself! On the contrary, an unrestrained capitalism would degenerate into fascism by crushing the democratic aspirations of the people.**

⚠️ **The reconciliation is elegant and should be reproduced.** Both agree on the **conditional**: capitalism that accommodates democratic aspirations does not become fascist. They differ on what accommodation **leads to** — Ebenstein treats it as capitalism's stable, healthy state; Laski treats it as a transition that, carried through, **transforms capitalism itself**. ⚠️ So the disagreement is about the destination, not the mechanism, and an answer that says so has resolved what looks like a contradiction.

### 5. The liberal and Marxist critiques

The chapter opens with its own characterisation and Laski's: **fascism is by no means a systematic doctrine. It is a queer mixture of incongruous elements.** Laski: fascism **proves to be nothing more than an ill-assorted rag-bag in which all kinds of remnants from the most diverse philosophies seek, as best they may, to find a place**. And then Mussolini's own 1924 admission:

> We Fascists have had the courage to discard all traditional political theories, and we are aristocrats and democrats, revolutionaries and reactionaries, proletarians and anti-proletarians, pacifists and antipacifists. It is sufficient to have a single fixed point: the nation. The rest is obvious.
>
> — Benito Mussolini, in an article written in 1924, as quoted by Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.71

⚠️ **This is the corroboration promised at Lesson 8**, and it should be deployed there and here. Four contradictory pairs are claimed simultaneously, with **a single fixed point: the nation** — which is the incoherence thesis confirmed by the doctrine's founder.

**The five liberal objections**, as the chapter numbers them:

| | Objection |
|---|---|
| 1 | fascism **sought to destroy individual liberty by subordinating individual to the absolute authority of the state, and by reducing individual to a means to serve the end of the state** |
| 2 | it **sought to accentuate the irrational element in human nature while liberalism pleads for man's freedom treating him primarily as a rational being** |
| 3 | it **repudiates the liberal faith in the natural and social equality of men, through its cult of hero-worship, superiority of the elite and racist doctrines** |
| 4 | it **hits at the pluralistic nature of society by establishing the monopoly of a single political party and eliminating free and open competition for political power** |
| 5 | it **demolishes constitutional government which is the sole guarantee of human freedom and progress as well as the cardinal principle of liberal-democracy** |

⚠️ Note that these five map exactly onto the six rows of Lesson 10's rejection table, which is why that table is worth memorising: it generates the liberal critique automatically.

⚠️ The chapter also records a complication worth stating: **while liberalism is primarily a philosophy of capitalism, fascism also sought to promote capitalism rather out of the way**, and **liberal writers have taken different positions on this issue** — some, like Ebenstein, holding that **the Marxist interpretation of fascism in terms of class (identifying fascism with capitalism in decay) is not borne out by the facts** because **fascism cuts across all social groups**, while **still others feel that fascism sought to distort the liberal and benevolent character of capitalism by dissociating it from democratic traditions and diverting it from the path of the welfare state**.

⚠️ **Scan-artefact disclosure.** The extraction prints **"identifyng"**; rendered *identifying* and catalogued.

**The Marxist critique**: Marxists **deprecate fascism as an attempt to protect capitalism in its decadent phase. By creating the myth of a nation, fascism sought to suppress class-conflict on the one hand, and to thwart any international movement toward communism on the other.** Two names follow:

- ✅ **Antonio Gramsci (1891-1937), an Italian Marxist who was victimized and tortured by the fascist regime of Mussolini, maintained that the ideological propaganda of the fascists sought to preserve capitalist 'hegemony' and 'structures of domination' which made it acceptable to the ignorant masses.**
- ✅ **Leon Trotsky (1879-1940) and others argued that the mass basis of fascism was provided by a desperate, rootless, middle class. The widespread fear of uncertainty in a time of crisis served to provide an authoritarian basis for fascism.**

⚠️ **Gramsci's appearance here is the direct bridge to Topic 04**, where **hegemony** and **structures of legitimation** were reconstructed from the power chapter at PDF pp.306, 309-311. ⚠️ Note that here the terms are **'hegemony'** and **'structures of domination'**, and that the application is specifically to **fascist** propaganda — which is a narrower and more pointed use than the general theory. An answer may cite both, provided the cross-chapter provenance of the fuller account is stated.

⚠️ **Trotsky's position revives the lower-middle-class thesis the chapter rejected at §1** — **a desperate, rootless, middle class**. ⚠️ The chapter does not reconcile its own rejection with Trotsky's endorsement. This session reports both and records the tension; an answer should attribute the thesis to **Trotsky** and note the chapter's own earlier scepticism.

### 6. The conclusion, and the refusal to equate

The chapter closes by rejecting a classification: **some liberal writers, prompted by their equal disdain for fascism and communism, have sought to club the two as 'totalitarian dictatorships'. For instance, R.M. MacIver (*The Web of Government*; 1965) and Alan Ball (*Modern Politics and Government*; 1988) have adopted this classification. This is not only unfair but misleading.**

The ground: **fascism tends to suppress the masses to secure the interests of a tiny class in all spheres—social, economic, political. On the contrary, communism—even when it uses coercion—seeks to distribute benefits to secure the maximum satisfaction of the masses.** And the supporting authority:

> Totalitarian systems suppress demands coming from their societies and are unresponsive to demands coming from the international environment. At the same time, they regulate and coerce behaviour in their societies, … totalitarianism differs from fascist totalitarianism in having a strong distributive capability as well.
>
> — G. A. Almond and G. B. Powell, *Comparative Politics: A Developmental Approach* (1966), as quoted by Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.73

⚠️ **Text-integrity note, stated before any use is made of the passage.** The local extraction of this quotation is **defective**: the sentence beginning **totalitarianism differs from fascist totalitarianism** lacks its subject, which the surrounding argument indicates should be a term designating communist totalitarian systems. ⚠️ The passage is reproduced above exactly as extracted, with an ellipsis marking the gap, and the defect is recorded in the Source Ledger. ⚠️ The **argument** is unaffected and is independently supplied by the chapter's own preceding sentences; only the quotation's completeness is at issue, and no reading in this session rests on the damaged clause.

⚠️ **The distinction being drawn is the chapter's most important classificatory ruling and is frequently examined.** Both are totalitarian in **suppressing demands** and **coercing behaviour**; they differ in **distributive capability**. ⚠️ Fascism suppresses **to secure the interests of a tiny class**; communism, **even when it uses coercion**, **seeks to distribute benefits to secure the maximum satisfaction of the masses**. The chapter is not defending communist coercion — the concessive **even when it uses coercion** marks that — but refusing an equation that ignores purpose.

The final verdict:

> Fascism, besides its retrograde class character, symbolizes a sick mental and political attitude. It fosters anti-human and anti-progressive forces. It seeks to curb liberty and equality and to distort justice. In short, it is a philosophy of the lunatic fringe, champions of terror and violence who advocate superiority of one race, sect, region, religion, language or culture, relegating the rest of mankind to slavery. Unfortunately, fascist tendencies are not dead the world over.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.73 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

📰 The closing observation that **fascist tendencies are not dead the world over** and that **enemies of mankind still thrive in some parts of the world** is a 2009 statement and is reported as such. ❌ No contemporary state, movement or party is named here or anywhere in this session.

Revision notes:
- Thesis A: **McGovern (*From Luther to Hitler*; 1941)** and **MacIver (*The Web of Government*; 1965)** — fascism as **a movement of the lower middle class**. The chapter: **does not seem to be correct**.
- Conceded: it **particularly appealed to small businessmen … threatened on the one hand by the rising working class … and on the other by the monster of monopoly capitalism**. Rejected: it **did not emanate from any movement launched by the lower middle class**; the **militia were recruited from a subclass of the tradeless dregs of the working class without class loyalty or self-respect**.
- The chapter's test: **determine which class it sought to serve** — ⚠️ a **functional** test.
- **Laski**: fascism **sought to serve the interests of the capitalist class**; **stripped of all its rhetorical trappings Italian fascism appears quite simply as an insistence upon compulsory obedience to a state whose purpose is to protect existing class-relations**.
- **Ebenstein**: **the conditions of capitalism do not by themselves give rise to fascism, but it arises only where democracy is particularly weak**; **industrialists are not, as a class, any more fascist-minded**, yet **it took only a few wealthy industrialists and landowners** to fund movements; depression produces a circle of mutual blame and **the large mass of unemployed people**; relief can meet income but not **the feeling of being useless, unwanted, and outside the productive ranks**; a **uniform** gives belonging and **a superior race or nation** restores **self-respect**.
- Reconciliation: both agree **so long as capitalism can accommodate the democratic aspirations of the people, it is not likely to degenerate into fascism**; they differ on whether accommodation is stable (Ebenstein) or transformative (Laski).
- Incoherence confirmed: Laski's **ill-assorted rag-bag**; Mussolini 1924 — **aristocrats and democrats, revolutionaries and reactionaries, proletarians and anti-proletarians, pacifists and antipacifists**, with **a single fixed point: the nation**.
- Five liberal objections: destruction of **individual liberty**; accentuating **the irrational element**; repudiating **natural and social equality**; attacking **the pluralistic nature of society**; demolishing **constitutional government**.
- Marxist critique: **an attempt to protect capitalism in its decadent phase**; **Gramsci (1891-1937)** on **'hegemony'** and **'structures of domination'**; **Trotsky (1879-1940)** on **a desperate, rootless, middle class**. ⚠️ Trotsky revives the thesis the chapter rejected — tension recorded.
- ❌ Refusal to equate: clubbing fascism and communism as **'totalitarian dictatorships'** is **not only unfair but misleading**; **Almond and Powell (1966)** distinguish by **distributive capability**. ⚠️ The extraction of that quotation is defective; the argument is independently supplied.
- Verdict: **a sick mental and political attitude**; **anti-human and anti-progressive**; it **seeks to curb liberty and equality and to distort justice**; **a philosophy of the lunatic fringe**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Laski says fascism served the capitalist class; Ebenstein says capitalism does not by itself produce fascism. Are they contradicting each other, and how does the chapter resolve it?

**Model answer:** They are not, because they answer different questions. Laski answers the chapter's own **functional** test — **determine which class it sought to serve** — and concludes that, **stripped of all its rhetorical trappings**, Italian fascism is **an insistence upon compulsory obedience to a state whose purpose is to protect existing class-relations** (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.70). Ebenstein answers a **causal** question and denies that capitalism is sufficient: **the conditions of capitalism do not by themselves give rise to fascism, but it arises only where democracy is particularly weak**, since **industrialists are not, as a class, any more fascist-minded than other social groups** — though **it took only a few wealthy industrialists and landowners to supply fascist movements with ample funds**. ⚠️ Benefit and causation are distinct, so both can hold: fascism served capital **and** was not produced by capitalism as such. The chapter resolves it explicitly, noting that Ebenstein's point is **not fundamentally different from Laski's view**, because both accept the conditional that **so long as capitalism can accommodate the democratic aspirations of the people, it is not likely to degenerate into fascism**. ⚠️ The residual disagreement is about the destination rather than the mechanism: Ebenstein treats accommodation as capitalism's stable healthy condition, while Laski holds that carrying **the marriage between capitalism and democracy** to its **logical conclusion** would **transform capitalism itself**, and that **unrestrained capitalism would degenerate into fascism**. ⚠️ An answer that separates the functional from the causal question, and then locates the real disagreement at the destination, has done more than report two views.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not use the chapter's refusal to equate fascism with communist totalitarianism as a defence of communist coercion. The chapter's own phrasing is concessive — communism **even when it uses coercion** — and the distinction it draws is about **purpose and distributive capability**, not about means. The ruling is that clubbing the two as **'totalitarian dictatorships'** is **unfair** and **misleading** because it ignores that fascism suppresses **to secure the interests of a tiny class**; it is not a ruling that one form of coercion is acceptable.

## Lesson 14 — What anarchism is: the target, and what is not the target

Progress: 14 / 24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Godwin and Proudhon, the one common aim, and the authority the chapter says anarchists keep

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§3, 5 and 9 with the Advanced companion's §4, read against the source chapter's section headed "What is Anarchism?" (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF pp.73-74), which supplies the definition, Godwin's priority and his 1793 work, Proudhon's self-naming, the period of greatest influence, the one common aim with its two disagreements, and the three kinds of authority anarchists retain or reject.
CA search: Not re-run for this lesson; see Lessons 1 and 13.
CA found: As reported there.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — what is rejected, and what survives**

```text
   THE DEFINITION                                    (PDF p.73)
   "Anarchism stands for a MODE OF THOUGHT which holds that
    society CAN and SHOULD be ORGANIZED WITHOUT THE COERCIVE
    AUTHORITY OF THE STATE."
                              |
   ONE COMMON AIM, TWO DISAGREEMENTS
   "Anarchist thinkers have ONE COMMON AIM: ABOLITION OF THE
    STATE. But they WIDELY DIFFER as to (i) HOW the state should
    be abolished, and (ii) WHAT TYPE OF ORGANIZATION should be
    evolved to REPLACE it. So they by no means form a
    HOMOGENEOUS GROUP."
   range: "from the defenders of EXTREME FORM OF SOCIALISM to the
    champions of EXTREME FORM OF INDIVIDUALISM"
                              |
   =============== THE AUTHORITY LEDGER =============== (p.74)
   REJECTED by all:
     "COMPULSORY FORMS OF AUTHORITY, culminating in AUTHORITY OF
      THE STATE"
   MOSTLY AVERSE TO:
     "HIERARCHICAL forms of authority, e.g. CHURCHES, ARMIES,
      BOSSES OF CAPITALIST ENTERPRISES, and IMPERSONAL
      BUREAUCRACIES"
   RETAINED by MOST anarchists:
     (a) "the RATIONAL FORM OF AUTHORITY, particularly the
          AUTHORITY OF EXPERTS, e.g. SCIENTISTS and DOCTORS in
          their respective fields"
     (b) "the MORAL AUTHORITY OF COLLECTIVE DECISIONS TAKEN IN A
          GENUINELY DEMOCRATIC MANNER"
   ====================================================
                              |
   THEREFORE: ANARCHISM IS NOT CHAOS. The target is COERCION,
   not COORDINATION.
```

Caption: the ledger is the whole lesson. Two kinds of authority are rejected and two are kept, which is what makes anarchism a political doctrine rather than a refusal of order.

### 1. The definition, and the word "should"

**Anarchism stands for a mode of thought which holds that society can and should be organized without the coercive authority of the state** (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.73).

⚠️ **Three precisions in one sentence.** It is a **mode of thought**, not a party or a programme. It asserts both possibility (**can**) and desirability (**should**) — so a critic must defeat both, and the chapter's own appraisal at Lesson 18 attacks the **can**. And the object is **the coercive authority of the state**, not authority as such.

### 2. The two founders, and their different kinds of priority

- ✅ **William Godwin (1756-1836), a British political theorist, was the first thinker who argued unequivocally for a stateless society.** **His Enquiry Concerning Political Justice (1793) is regarded to be the first systematic defence of anarchism.**
- ✅ **However, it was P.J. Proudhon (1809-65), a French philosopher, who was the first to call himself an anarchist.**

⚠️ **The two priorities are of different kinds and the distinction is examinable.** Godwin's is **doctrinal** — the first systematic defence. Proudhon's is **nominal** — the first self-description. ⚠️ So anarchism as an argued position precedes anarchism as a named identity by roughly half a century, which is a neat way of making the point that the doctrine was not invented by its label.

⚠️ The chapter also notes that **some indications of this mode of thought could be traced to ancient times** — a hedge that stops short of naming any ancient source. ❌ Do not supply one.

📰 **As an ideology anarchism had its greatest influence in late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, when several revolutionary movements in Western countries favoured this mode of thought.** A dated claim, reported as such.

### 3. The one aim and the two disagreements

> Anarchist thinkers have one common aim: abolition of the state. But they widely differ as to how the state should be abolished, and what type of organization should be evolved to replace it. So they by no means form a homogeneous group. They include a wide variety of thinkers ranging from the defenders of extreme form of socialism to the champions of extreme form of individualism.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.73 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **This structure — one aim, two axes of disagreement — is the map of Lessons 15-18 and should be used to organise any answer on anarchism's varieties.** The **how** axis separates peaceful from insurrectionary schools (Proudhon and Tolstoy against Bakunin); the **what replaces it** axis separates mutualist from communist from collectivist from syndicalist from minimal-state answers.

⚠️ **The range statement is the most striking fact about anarchism and the one that makes the 2026 examination stem intelligible.** A family stretching **from the defenders of extreme form of socialism to the champions of extreme form of individualism** spans the entire left-right axis of the other doctrines in this topic. ⚠️ That is precisely why a question can ask whether anarchism is an independent ideology at all or a confluence of liberalism and socialism — and the chapter's own range statement is the best evidence on both sides.

### 4. The authority ledger — the heart of the lesson

The chapter's sentence is that **all anarchists agree on the need to dispense with compulsory forms of authority, culminating in authority of the state**. Then the crucial qualification:

> However, most anarchists recognize the rational form of authority, particularly the authority of experts, e.g. scientists and doctors in their respective fields, as also the moral authority of collective decisions taken in a genuinely democratic manner. They are mostly averse to hierarchical forms of authority, e.g. churches, armies, bosses of capitalist enterprises, and impersonal bureaucracies.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.74 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Scan-artefact disclosure.** The extraction prints **"hiearchical"**; rendered *hierarchical* and catalogued.

⚠️ **This passage is the answer to the single commonest misconception about anarchism and should be quoted whenever that misconception is in play.** Four categories are distinguished:

| Category | Status | The chapter's examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Compulsory** authority, culminating in the state | **rejected by all** | the state |
| **Hierarchical** authority | **most are averse** | **churches, armies, bosses of capitalist enterprises, and impersonal bureaucracies** |
| **Rational** authority | **most recognise** | **scientists and doctors in their respective fields** |
| **Moral** authority of collective decisions | **most recognise** | decisions **taken in a genuinely democratic manner** |

⚠️ **Two observations lift this above a list.**

1. **The retained categories are defined by their ground, not by their subject.** Rational authority is recognised **in their respective fields** — competence-bounded. Moral authority attaches to decisions **taken in a genuinely democratic manner** — procedure-bounded. ⚠️ What anarchism rejects is authority that is **unbounded by either competence or consent**, which is a principled position rather than a temperamental one.
2. **The hierarchical list is instructive because of what it contains.** **Churches, armies, bosses of capitalist enterprises, and impersonal bureaucracies** — only one of which is the state. ⚠️ So anarchism's target is broader than government: it is a structural objection to command-relations wherever they occur. That connects directly to Lesson 16's Proudhon, for whom **power of the state and power of capital are coterminous**.

⚠️ Note the chapter's hedges throughout — **most anarchists**, **mostly averse**. The ledger describes a tendency, not a definition, and preserving the hedges is accurate.

### 5. The six schools announced

The chapter closes the section by naming what follows: **of the several schools of anarchist thought, the following are particularly important: philosophical anarchism, socialist anarchism, revolutionary anarchism, anarcho-syndicalism, pacific anarchism, and libertarian anarchism.**

⚠️ Note that **socialist anarchism** is treated as the trunk from which two others branch: Lesson 17 records that **revolutionary anarchism is still another version of socialist anarchism** and that **anarcho-syndicalism … is another version of revolutionary anarchism**. ⚠️ So the six are not six parallel items but a structure — and reproducing the structure rather than the list is what shows comprehension.

Revision notes:
- **Anarchism stands for a mode of thought which holds that society can and should be organized without the coercive authority of the state.** ⚠️ **Can** and **should** are separate claims.
- **William Godwin (1756-1836), a British political theorist**, was **the first thinker who argued unequivocally for a stateless society**; his ***Enquiry Concerning Political Justice* (1793)** is **the first systematic defence of anarchism**. **P.J. Proudhon (1809-65), a French philosopher**, was **the first to call himself an anarchist**.
- ⚠️ Doctrinal priority (Godwin) and nominal priority (Proudhon) are different things.
- 📰 Greatest influence **in late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries**.
- **One common aim: abolition of the state**, with wide difference on **how the state should be abolished** and **what type of organization should be evolved to replace it**; **by no means … a homogeneous group**, ranging **from the defenders of extreme form of socialism to the champions of extreme form of individualism**.
- The authority ledger: **all** reject **compulsory forms of authority, culminating in authority of the state**; **most are averse to hierarchical forms** — **churches, armies, bosses of capitalist enterprises, and impersonal bureaucracies**; **most recognize** the **rational form of authority, particularly the authority of experts, e.g. scientists and doctors in their respective fields**, and **the moral authority of collective decisions taken in a genuinely democratic manner**.
- ⚠️ Retained authority is bounded by **competence** or by **consent**; rejected authority is unbounded by either.
- ⚠️ Only one of the four hierarchical examples is the state — the objection is to command-relations generally.
- Six schools: **philosophical, socialist, revolutionary, anarcho-syndicalism, pacific, libertarian** — ⚠️ a **structure**, since revolutionary anarchism is a version of socialist anarchism and anarcho-syndicalism a version of revolutionary anarchism.

### Concept check

**Question:** "Anarchism means chaos." Refute this from the chapter's own text, and then state what the refutation does **not** establish.

**Model answer:** The refutation is a single passage. The chapter records that while all anarchists reject **compulsory forms of authority, culminating in authority of the state**, **most anarchists recognize the rational form of authority, particularly the authority of experts, e.g. scientists and doctors in their respective fields, as also the moral authority of collective decisions taken in a genuinely democratic manner** (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.74). ⚠️ Two retained categories are therefore explicit, and both are **principled** rather than residual: rational authority is bounded by **competence** — recognised **in their respective fields** — and moral authority is bounded by **procedure**, attaching to decisions **taken in a genuinely democratic manner**. What anarchism rejects is authority unbounded by either, which is why the hierarchical list names **churches, armies, bosses of capitalist enterprises, and impersonal bureaucracies** alongside the state. The definition itself says society **can and should be organized** — organisation is affirmed, not denied. ⚠️ What the refutation does **not** establish is that the arrangement would work. That is a separate question, and the chapter presses it hard in its own appraisal at Lesson 18: anarchism **takes a too optimistic view of human nature**, and if human nature were **so benign as envisaged by anarchists**, the ills the anarchists want to remedy **would not have arisen at all**. ⚠️ So the correct structure for an answer is two-stage: anarchism is **not** chaos, because the authority ledger is explicit and principled; but its **feasibility** is attacked on grounds entirely independent of the chaos charge, and conflating the two lets a candidate win the easy argument and miss the real one.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the six schools as a flat list. The chapter builds them structurally — revolutionary anarchism is **still another version of socialist anarchism**, and anarcho-syndicalism is **another version of revolutionary anarchism**. Reproducing the nesting shows that the varieties have a logic, and it makes the individualist/socialist range statement at PDF p.73 intelligible rather than merely surprising.

## Lesson 15 — Philosophical / individualist anarchism

Progress: 15 / 24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Godwin, Stirner and Wolff — can anyone be entitled to command another?

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§5 and 6 with the Advanced companion's §4, read against the source chapter's section headed "Philosophical Anarchism" (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.74), which supplies the denial of legitimate authority, the autonomy premise, the individualist label, Godwin's and Stirner's positions with dates and works, Wolff's contemporary endorsement, the cooperation limitation, and the contracts-should-be-kept and commune qualifications.
CA search: Not re-run for this lesson; see Lessons 1 and 13.
CA found: As reported there.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — the argument, and the exceptions it still allows**

```text
   THE CORE DENIAL                                   (PDF p.74)
   "Philosophical anarchism REJECTS THE IDEA OF LEGITIMATE
    AUTHORITY in the sense that NO INDIVIDUAL, WHETHER STATE
    OFFICIAL OR NOT, has the RIGHT TO COMMAND THE OBEDIENCE OF
    ANOTHER."
                              |
   THE PREMISE
   "INDIVIDUAL AUTONOMY, as conceived MORALLY, requires
    individuals to ACT ACCORDING TO THEIR OWN JUDGMENTS."
                              |
   = also called 'INDIVIDUALIST ANARCHISM' ("because of its focus
     on individual")
                              |
   THE LINE OF DESCENT
   GODWIN (Enquiry Concerning Political Justice; 1793) -- founder
        |
   MAX STIRNER (1806-56), German philosopher
   The Ego and his Own (1845): "every individual is the UNIQUE
   ONE who truly 'owns himself'; he recognizes NO DUTIES TO
   OTHERS, and does WHAT IS RIGHT FOR HIMSELF, WITHIN THE LIMIT
   OF HIS MIGHT"
        |
   R.P. WOLFF (In Defense of Anarchism; 1970) -- endorses
   Godwin's line "in contemporary thought"
                              |
   THE CONSEQUENCE THE CHAPTER DRAWS
   "philosophical anarchism has LITTLE SCOPE to encourage
    COOPERATION among individuals or to evolve their FORMAL
    ORGANIZATION"
                              |
   BUT THREE THINGS SURVIVE EVEN HERE
   (1) "the RATIONAL AUTHORITY OF EXPERTS within their fields of
        competence"
   (2) "the MORAL AUTHORITY OF BASIC SOCIAL NORMS, such as
        'CONTRACTS SHOULD BE KEPT'"
   (3) if politics is "the art of PERSUADING OTHERS when they do
        not agree", even POLITICAL AUTHORITY -- "but NOT THE
        COERCIVE AUTHORITY OF THE STATE"; so decisions of a
        COMMUNE or WORKERS' COOPERATIVE in which members
        "ACTUALLY PARTICIPATE in decision-making ... may be
        deemed MORALLY BINDING"
```

Caption: the most radical school in the family still keeps three kinds of authority. That is the measure of how far the "anarchism = chaos" charge misses.

### 1. The core denial, and why it is more radical than anti-statism

**Philosophical anarchism rejects the idea of legitimate authority in the sense that no individual, whether state official or not, has the right to command the obedience of another** (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.74).

⚠️ **The clause "whether state official or not" is what makes this school distinct.** Most anarchism targets the state; this school targets the **command-relation itself**, wherever it occurs. ⚠️ So philosophical anarchism is not primarily a theory about government; it is a theory about what one person can owe another, and the state becomes a target only as the largest instance.

The premise: **individual autonomy, as conceived morally, requires individuals to act according to their own judgments.**

⚠️ **This is a moral-autonomy argument and the structure is worth naming.** If acting rightly means acting on one's own judgment, then obeying a command *because it is a command* substitutes another's judgment for one's own, and is therefore inconsistent with moral agency. ⚠️ Note that this leaves room for doing what an authority says **because one judges it right** — which is exactly why the three exceptions at §4 are consistent rather than ad hoc.

**Because of its focus on individual, this school of thought is also called 'individualist anarchism'.**

### 2. The three named thinkers

- ✅ **Godwin.** **It was originally founded by Godwin himself in his essay Enquiry Concerning Political Justice (1793).** ⚠️ Note that the chapter calls it an **essay** here and **His *Enquiry*** at PDF p.73 — a minor inconsistency of description, recorded.
- ✅ **Max Stirner (1806-56), a German philosopher, in The Ego and his Own (1845) argued that every individual is the unique one who truly 'owns himself'; he recognizes no duties to others, and does what is right for himself, within the limit of his might.**
- ✅ **In contemporary thought, Godwin's line of argument was endorsed by R.P. Wolff (In Defense of Anarchism; 1970).**

⚠️ **Verified variance on Stirner, recorded.** The standard record, independently confirmed in this session, is that Stirner's work was published in German as *Der Einzige und sein Eigentum* in **1844**, with *The Ego and Its Own* as the usual English title. The chapter prints **The Ego and his Own (1845)**. ⚠️ Both the year and the pronoun differ from the standard form. The chapter's printing is reproduced where the chapter is being reported; an answer naming the work in its own voice should prefer the standard title, and should not assert a year without checking which edition is meant.

⚠️ **Scan-artefact disclosure.** The extraction prints **"truly 'owns himself;"** with a semicolon in place of the closing quotation mark; rendered **truly 'owns himself'** and catalogued.

⚠️ **Stirner's position is the most extreme in the whole topic and should be stated at full strength rather than softened.** Three claims: the individual **truly owns himself**; **recognizes no duties to others**; and does **what is right for himself, within the limit of his might**. ⚠️ The third is the dangerous one — the only constraint named is **might**, not right. ⚠️ That is worth saying plainly, because it shows the individualist end of the anarchist range reaching a position that most anarchists would reject and that sits uncomfortably beside the authority ledger of Lesson 14. The chapter does not reconcile them; this session reports the tension.

### 3. The consequence the chapter draws

**Accordingly, philosophical anarchism has little scope to encourage cooperation among individuals or to evolve their formal organization.**

⚠️ **This is a limitation the chapter itself identifies, and it is the right thing to concede in an answer.** A doctrine built on each person acting on **their own judgments** has no principled basis for binding coordination, which is precisely what large-scale cooperation requires. ⚠️ Compare Lesson 16's socialist anarchism, which supplies elaborate coordinating institutions — mutual credit banks, federated associations — and Lesson 18's appraisal, which charges all anarchism with inadequacy **to deal with such gigantic problems** as **worldwide terrorism, crime and environmental pollution**. The philosophical school is the most exposed to that charge.

### 4. The three survivals

Even here, the chapter records three forms of authority that survive:

> Its upholders are generally suspicious of authority, yet they recognize the rational authority of experts within their fields of competence and the moral authority of basic social norms, such as 'contracts should be kept'. If politics is defined as the art of persuading others when they do not agree, then philosophical anarchists may also recognize even political authority, but not the coercive authority of the state. So, if members of a commune or workers' cooperative actually participate in decision-making, their decisions may be deemed morally binding.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.74 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **The third survival is the most interesting and the least noticed.** The chapter offers a **definition of politics** — **the art of persuading others when they do not agree** — and observes that on that definition philosophical anarchists can accept **political authority** while rejecting **the coercive authority of the state**. ⚠️ The distinction is between authority exercised through **persuasion** and authority exercised through **coercion**, and it is the cleanest formulation of the anarchist position available anywhere in this topic.

⚠️ **The commune example makes it concrete and supplies the operative condition.** Decisions **may be deemed morally binding** where members **actually participate in decision-making**. ⚠️ Note the word **actually** — participation must be real, not formal. That is the same condition the authority ledger attached to **collective decisions taken in a genuinely democratic manner**, and the repetition shows it is doing systematic work.

⚠️ **How this answers Stirner.** Stirner **recognizes no duties to others**; the commune passage recognises morally binding collective decisions. ⚠️ The chapter places both inside philosophical anarchism without comment, which means the school contains a genuine internal range from Stirner's egoism to a participatory position close to democratic self-government. An answer that notices the range is more accurate than one that treats the school as uniform.

Revision notes:
- **Philosophical anarchism rejects the idea of legitimate authority in the sense that no individual, whether state official or not, has the right to command the obedience of another.** ⚠️ The target is the **command-relation**, not merely the state.
- Premise: **individual autonomy, as conceived morally, requires individuals to act according to their own judgments**. Also called **'individualist anarchism'**.
- **Godwin**, ***Enquiry Concerning Political Justice* (1793)** — founder. ⚠️ Called an **essay** here, **His *Enquiry*** at PDF p.73.
- **Max Stirner (1806-56), a German philosopher**, ***The Ego and his Own* (1845)**: the individual is **the unique one who truly 'owns himself'**; **recognizes no duties to others**; does **what is right for himself, within the limit of his might**. ⚠️ Verified variance — the standard record is *Der Einzige und sein Eigentum*, **1844**, usually rendered *The Ego and Its Own*.
- **R.P. Wolff**, ***In Defense of Anarchism*; 1970** — endorses Godwin's line **in contemporary thought**.
- The limitation: **little scope to encourage cooperation among individuals or to evolve their formal organization**.
- Three survivals: **the rational authority of experts within their fields of competence**; **the moral authority of basic social norms, such as 'contracts should be kept'**; and, if politics is **the art of persuading others when they do not agree**, even **political authority, but not the coercive authority of the state** — so a **commune or workers' cooperative** whose members **actually participate in decision-making** may have **morally binding** decisions.
- ⚠️ The persuasion/coercion distinction is the cleanest statement of the anarchist position in the topic.
- ⚠️ Internal range recorded: Stirner's **no duties to others** sits inside the same school as morally binding participatory decisions; the chapter does not reconcile them.

### Concept check

**Question:** Philosophical anarchism denies that anyone has a right to command another, yet accepts that a commune's decisions may be morally binding. Is that inconsistent?

**Model answer:** No, because the two claims concern different grounds of obligation. The denial is specifically that **no individual, whether state official or not, has the right to command the obedience of another** (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.74) — it targets obedience owed **because a command has been issued**, which would substitute another's judgment for one's own and so violate the premise that **individual autonomy, as conceived morally, requires individuals to act according to their own judgments**. The commune case has a different structure: where members **actually participate in decision-making**, the resulting decision is not an external command but an outcome the member's own judgment helped produce and to which they are party. ⚠️ The chapter makes the distinction explicit through its definition of politics as **the art of persuading others when they do not agree**, on which philosophical anarchists **may also recognize even political authority, but not the coercive authority of the state**. Authority grounded in **persuasion and participation** leaves moral agency intact; authority grounded in **coercion** does not. ⚠️ Two qualifications complete the answer. The operative condition is strict — members must **actually participate**, which rules out merely formal consent and is the same requirement the authority ledger imposed on **collective decisions taken in a genuinely democratic manner**. And the school nonetheless contains a position that sits awkwardly with this: Stirner **recognizes no duties to others** and acts **within the limit of his might**, which the chapter records without reconciling, so a precise answer should present philosophical anarchism as internally ranged rather than uniform.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat Stirner as representative of anarchism generally, or soften his position to make it fit. The chapter states it at full strength — **no duties to others**, constrained only by **might** — and it marks the extreme individualist pole of the range announced at PDF p.73. Most anarchists, on the chapter's own authority ledger, recognise rational and moral authority that Stirner's position excludes.

Progress: 16 / 24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Freedom as capacity, the federal principle, the mutual credit bank, and the refutation of survival of the fittest

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§6, 7, 8 and 16 Unit 6 with the Advanced companion's §5, read against the source chapter's section headed "Socialist Anarchism" (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF pp.74-75), which supplies the freedom-as-capacity definition, the double rejection of property and state, Proudhon's four theses, the federal and reward principles, the mutual credit bank, the record of practical failure, the First International date, and Kropotkin's argument against Darwin with his work and dates.

CA found: As reported there.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — two socialist-anarchist systems compared**

| | **PROUDHON — mutualism** | **KROPOTKIN — communist anarchism** |
|---|---|---|
| Dates and description | **P.J. Proudhon (1809-65), a French philosopher**, **chief exponent of socialist anarchism** | **Peter Kropotkin (1842-1921), a Russian thinker** |
| Named work | — (the chapter names none here) | ***Mutual Aid—a Factor of Evolution* (1890-96)** |
| Core method | **'mutual aid' as the appropriate method** — hence **'mutualism'** | **sociability** as **the natural condition of all evolutionary beings** |
| Key propositions | **liberty or freedom is the mother, not the daughter, of order**; **all political parties are a variety of despotism**; **power of the state and power of capital are coterminous**; hence **the proletariat cannot emancipate itself by acquiring and using state power** | **the struggle for existence and survival of the fittest** as enunciated by **Charles Darwin (1809-82)** **does not apply to the sphere of social relations**; **sociability is, under all circumstances, the greatest advantage in the struggle for life** |
| Target of criticism | **Marx's idea of establishing 'dictatorship of the proletariat' as a way to human emancipation** | the Darwinian extension to society |
| Method of change | **the (peaceful) method of direct action and the practice of mutualism** | if people **are not corrupted by the state and law**, they **would develop bonds of instinctive solidarity which would make government unnecessary** |
| Institutional form | **a network of autonomous local communities and producer associations, linked by 'the federal principle'** | **a system of 'communism' where everything belongs to everyone** |
| Property and reward | each may possess means of production **singly or collectively**, but **should only be rewarded for his labour**; eliminates **profit and rent** | **distribution is made according to needs** |
| Exchange | **each party will seek only an equivalent for what it offered**, plus a **mutual credit bank** lending at **a minimal rate of interest, covering only its cost of administration** | — |

Caption: both reject state and capital together; they diverge on the reward principle — **labour** for Proudhon, **needs** for Kropotkin.

### 1. The school's definition

**Socialist anarchism insists on freedom of individual, defined as the capacity to satisfy his needs. It regards social and economic equality as a necessary condition to secure maximum freedom of all. In its view, social and economic equality is incompatible with capitalist private property and the state. It therefore rejects both** (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.74).

⚠️ **Freedom is defined as a capacity, and that single move organises the school.** If freedom is **the capacity to satisfy his needs**, then a person without resources is unfree regardless of what no one is preventing — which is the positive-liberty structure Topic 03 identified in welfare liberalism, deployed here to an anti-state conclusion. ⚠️ Equality then follows as **a necessary condition** of maximum freedom rather than as a competing value.

⚠️ **The double rejection is what makes it anarchism rather than socialism.** Equality is **incompatible with capitalist private property and the state**, so **it therefore rejects both**. ⚠️ Compare Lesson 2's evolutionary socialism, which rejects only the first, and Lesson 6's guild socialism, which rejects the state under a description while retaining a successor body.

### 2. Proudhon's four propositions

The chapter attributes four to him, and each repays a sentence:

- ✅ **Liberty or freedom is the mother, not the daughter, of order.** ⚠️ The inversion is the point: order is usually presented as the precondition of liberty, so that liberty must wait for order to be established. Proudhon reverses the dependency — liberty **generates** order. ⚠️ That is the proposition the chapter's appraisal at Lesson 18 attacks when it says anarchism **takes a too optimistic view of human nature**, and it is also, structurally, the claim Topic 03's neoliberal spontaneous order makes from a very different political position.
- ✅ **All political parties are a variety of despotism.** ⚠️ Note how far this reaches: not merely the ruling party but **all** parties, which rules out the Lassallean and Fabian routes of Lessons 3-5 at a stroke.
- ✅ **Power of the state and power of capital are coterminous.** ⚠️ **Coterminous** — having the same boundaries, not merely allied. This is the sharpest single sentence in the anarchist arc, because it means an attack on one that leaves the other standing has attacked nothing.
- ✅ Hence **the proletariat cannot emancipate itself by acquiring and using state power**. **On this ground Proudhon criticized Marx's idea of establishing 'dictatorship of the proletariat' as a way to human emancipation.**

⚠️ **The fourth proposition is a deduction from the third and should be presented as one.** If state power and capital power are coterminous, then capturing the state is capturing a form of capital power, and the captor becomes what was to be abolished. ⚠️ This is the most serious anarchist objection to Marxism in the entire topic, and it is an **internal** objection — it accepts the diagnosis of capitalist domination and attacks the proposed remedy.

### 3. Proudhon's institutional design

**Instead of violent method of overthrowing capitalism, Proudhon recommended the (peaceful) method of direct action and the practice of mutualism for evolving a new social order.**

The design has four elements:

1. ✅ **Society should be organized as a network of autonomous local communities and producer associations, linked by 'the federal principle'.**
2. ✅ **Each person might possess his means of production (tools, land, etc.) either singly or collectively, but should only be rewarded for his labour.**
3. ✅ **This system will eliminate the elements of profit and rent, and ensure a high degree of equality.**
4. ✅ **Exchange of goods and services between different voluntary associations will be based on the principle that each party will seek only an equivalent for what it offered to the other.** Supplemented by **the establishment of a mutual credit bank which would lend to producers at a minimal rate of interest, covering only its cost of administration**.

⚠️ **Element 2 is a careful and under-noticed distinction.** Possession of means of production is permitted — **singly or collectively** — while **reward** is restricted to labour. ⚠️ So mutualism is not abolition of property but separation of **possession** from **unearned return**, which is why it eliminates **profit and rent** specifically rather than ownership as such.

⚠️ **Element 4 is the most concrete institutional proposal in the anarchist arc and should be named.** A bank lending at a rate **covering only its cost of administration** removes interest as a source of unearned income while preserving credit as a function. ⚠️ Together with the equivalence principle in exchange, it shows that mutualism has a worked-out economic mechanism, not merely an aspiration — which is the right reply to a charge that anarchism is purely negative.

⚠️ **The chapter records the outcome honestly, and so should an answer.** **Proudhon's experiments on these lines proved to be a failure in actual practice. But his French disciples played an influential role in the early years of the First International (founded by the London Working Men's Association in 1864).**

⚠️ **Variance recorded.** The chapter attributes the First International's founding to **the London Working Men's Association in 1864**. The standard record is that the **International Workingmen's Association** was founded in London in 1864; the chapter's naming of the founding body differs from the usual form. ⚠️ The year is uncontroversial; the chapter's phrasing is reproduced where the chapter is reported, and the variance is recorded in the Source Ledger rather than resolved here.

### 4. Kropotkin against Darwin

**Philosophy of socialist anarchism was further developed by Peter Kropotkin (1842-1921), a Russian thinker. In Mutual Aid—a Factor of Evolution (1890-96), Kropotkin argued that the principle of 'the struggle for existence and survival of the fittest' as enunciated by Charles Darwin (1809-82) does not apply to the sphere of social relations. On the contrary, sociability is, under all circumstances, the greatest advantage in the struggle for life, and therefore the natural condition of all evolutionary beings.**

⚠️ **The argument's form is worth naming because it is cleverer than it first appears.** Kropotkin does not reject Darwinian struggle; he **accepts the criterion and reverses the result**. If the test is **advantage in the struggle for life**, then **sociability is, under all circumstances, the greatest advantage** — so cooperation wins on competition's own terms. ⚠️ That is an internal refutation, and internal refutations are the strongest kind.

The political conclusion: **if human beings are not corrupted by the state and law, they would develop bonds of instinctive solidarity which would make government unnecessary.**

⚠️ **Note the causal direction, which is the heart of the anarchist case and the point of maximum vulnerability.** The state and law are presented not as remedies for a defective human nature but as the **corrupters** of a sound one. ⚠️ That is exactly the claim the chapter's appraisal at Lesson 18 turns around: if human nature were as benign as anarchists suppose, **the ills afflicting human society under the state would not have arisen at all**. ⚠️ Stating both the claim and the chapter's rejoinder is what makes an answer on anarchism critical rather than expository.

**Kropotkin favoured a system of 'communism' where everything belongs to everyone, and distribution is made according to needs. Kropotkin's version of socialist anarchism is called 'communist anarchism'.**

⚠️ **The needs principle marks the divergence from Proudhon and should be stated as a disagreement within the school.** Proudhon rewards **labour**; Kropotkin distributes according to **needs**. ⚠️ These are different principles of justice and they can conflict — a person who needs much and labours little is treated differently under each. ⚠️ Note too that Kropotkin's formula is close to the Marxian one the chapter reports at Lesson 22 — **from each according to his ability, to each according to his need** — which shows how near the socialist-anarchist pole comes to Marxism on distribution while remaining opposed on the state.

Revision notes:
- **Socialist anarchism insists on freedom of individual, defined as the capacity to satisfy his needs**; **social and economic equality as a necessary condition to secure maximum freedom of all**; equality is **incompatible with capitalist private property and the state**, so **it therefore rejects both**.
- **P.J. Proudhon (1809-65), a French philosopher**, **chief exponent of socialist anarchism**; **'mutual aid' as the appropriate method**, hence **'mutualism'**.
- Four propositions: **liberty or freedom is the mother, not the daughter, of order**; **all political parties are a variety of despotism**; **power of the state and power of capital are coterminous**; therefore **the proletariat cannot emancipate itself by acquiring and using state power** — the ground on which he **criticized Marx's idea of establishing 'dictatorship of the proletariat'**.
- Design: **the (peaceful) method of direct action**; **a network of autonomous local communities and producer associations, linked by 'the federal principle'**; possession **singly or collectively** but reward **only … for his labour**, eliminating **profit and rent**; exchange on **an equivalent for what it offered**; a **mutual credit bank … at a minimal rate of interest, covering only its cost of administration**.
- ⚠️ Mutualism separates **possession** from **unearned return** rather than abolishing property.
- **Proudhon's experiments on these lines proved to be a failure in actual practice**; his disciples influenced **the First International (founded by the London Working Men's Association in 1864)**. ⚠️ Naming variance recorded; the standard form is the International Workingmen's Association, London, 1864.
- **Peter Kropotkin (1842-1921), a Russian thinker**, ***Mutual Aid—a Factor of Evolution* (1890-96)**: **'the struggle for existence and survival of the fittest' as enunciated by Charles Darwin (1809-82) does not apply to the sphere of social relations**; **sociability is, under all circumstances, the greatest advantage in the struggle for life, and therefore the natural condition of all evolutionary beings**.
- ⚠️ An **internal** refutation — it accepts the criterion and reverses the result.
- **If human beings are not corrupted by the state and law, they would develop bonds of instinctive solidarity which would make government unnecessary.** ⚠️ The state as **corrupter**, not remedy — the claim Lesson 18's appraisal inverts.
- **'Communism' where everything belongs to everyone, and distribution … according to needs** = **'communist anarchism'**.
- ⚠️ Proudhon rewards **labour**, Kropotkin distributes by **needs** — a real disagreement within the school.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is Proudhon's claim that "power of the state and power of capital are coterminous" the most damaging anarchist objection to Marxism in this chapter?

**Model answer:** Because it attacks the Marxist remedy using the Marxist diagnosis, which makes it an internal objection rather than an external disagreement. Proudhon accepts that capitalist domination is the problem; what he denies is that the state is a neutral instrument that the oppressed can seize and redirect. If **power of the state and power of capital are coterminous** — sharing the same boundaries rather than merely being allied — then the state is not an instrument lying beside capital but the same power in another form, and it follows, as the chapter records, that **the proletariat cannot emancipate itself by acquiring and using state power** (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.75). ⚠️ On that basis Proudhon **criticized Marx's idea of establishing 'dictatorship of the proletariat' as a way to human emancipation**: a transitional seizure of state power would not be a step toward abolishing domination but an assumption of it. ⚠️ The objection is damaging in two further ways. It generalises — **all political parties are a variety of despotism** — which simultaneously rules out the Fabian and Lassallean parliamentary routes of Lessons 3-5, so it is not merely anti-Marxist but anti-political in the party sense. And it is partly corroborated by the chapter's own later material: Topic 04 recorded that in socialist states there was **not 'withering away of the state' but a collapse of socialist state**, an outcome at least consistent with Proudhon's prediction. ⚠️ The honest residual is that coterminous is asserted, not argued — the chapter gives no demonstration that the boundaries of state power and capital power actually coincide, and a Marxist reply would be that they coincide under capitalism precisely because the state is a class instrument, which is why changing the class that holds it changes what it is.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat Proudhon and Kropotkin as holding one position because both are socialist anarchists. They differ on the principle of distribution — Proudhon holds that a person **should only be rewarded for his labour**, while Kropotkin's communism distributes **according to needs** — and these can conflict directly. Collapsing them also loses Proudhon's distinctive separation of **possession** from unearned return, which is what makes mutualism an economic design rather than a slogan.

## Lesson 17 — Revolutionary, syndicalist and pacific anarchism: Bakunin, Sorel, Tolstoy

Progress: 17 / 24 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Three methods for one goal, and the classification caution about Sorel

🖼️ **VISUAL — three methods, ranged by their attitude to violence**

    EXPLOITING OTHERS' LABOUR. BOTH of them should be ABOLISHED

      INSPIRED BY THESE IDEAS in developing his philosophy of
      non-violence."
