🖼️ **VISUAL — the three discriminating questions**

```text
   Q1: DOES THE DOCTRINE SEEK EQUALITY?
       YES -> socialism | anarchism | Gandhism
       NO, it seeks HIERARCHY -> FASCISM
       NO DESIGNED END-STATE at all -> CONSERVATISM
                              |
   Q2: WHAT IS THE STATE FOR?
       socialism .......... an INSTRUMENT of reform
       anarchism .......... to be ABOLISHED (libertarians:
                            MINIMISED, not abolished)
       Gandhism ........... "a SOULLESS MACHINE for coercion",
                            to become REDUNDANT
       fascism ............ an END IN ITSELF; the individual a
                            MEANS
       conservatism ....... a historically grown INHERITANCE to
                            be conserved and REPAIRED
                              |
   Q3: HOW DOES CHANGE HAPPEN?
       socialism .......... PARLIAMENT, planning, unions
                            (evolutionary) / REVOLUTION
                            (revolutionary)
       anarchism .......... VOLUNTARY FEDERATION | INSURRECTION |
                            GENERAL STRIKE | DISOBEDIENCE
       Gandhism ........... SATYAGRAHA + a constructive programme
       fascism ............ the LEADER and the SINGLE PARTY,
                            backed by MILITIA and PROPAGANDA
       conservatism ....... PARTICULAR, EVIDENCED, REVERSIBLE
                            repair of a demonstrated malfunction
```

Caption: three questions settle almost every classification problem this topic can set. Q1 is the one that keeps fascism out of the egalitarian family.

### 1. The master synthesis table

| Axis | **Democratic socialism** | **Fascism** | **Anarchism** | **Gandhism** | 🔶 **Conservatism** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Liberty** | Meaningful only once economic life is reorganised — political liberty is otherwise **an empty form** | **Subordinated entirely**; **true liberty … found only in subjection to state authority** | Freedom from **coercive** authority, not from rational or moral authority | **Ahimsa comes before swaraj**; freedom inseparable from self-discipline and truth | A condition secured by settled order; endangered by abstract redesign |
| **Equality** | Through **public control** of the major means of production | **Rejected**; **hierarchy culminating in a supreme leader** | Through abolishing coercive hierarchy — mutualist, communist or collectivist | Through **trusteeship** and **bread labour**, not coercive levelling | Not a goal; **widely spread property** as a stabiliser |
| **State** | **An instrument of reform** | **An end in itself**; the individual a **means** | To be **abolished** (⚠️ libertarians: minimised) | **A soulless machine for coercion**, to become redundant | An inheritance to be **conserved and repaired** |
| **Property** | **Ownership and control of public authority … to secure the public interest** | Left **concentrated**, stripped of democratic restraint | Mutualist / needs-based / collectivist — varies by school | **Trusteeship**: custodianship, not expropriation | A **stake in the order**; a sphere independent of the state |
| **Method** | Parliament, planning, unions, permeation | **Single-party dictatorship**, myth, suppression | Federation, insurrection, general strike or disobedience | **Satyagraha** plus constructive programme | **Particular, evidenced, reversible** repair |
| **Verdict on it in this session** | **An effective instrument for mitigating the rigours of capitalism**, failing **for want of adequate, organized and consistent public pressure** | **A sick mental and political attitude**; **not** a genuine theory | **A fascinating idea** but **too optimistic** about human nature and inadequate at scale | Trusteeship's **change of heart** is **hardly realizable in actual practice** | Second-order only; cannot choose **between competing traditions** |

### 2. The five standing confusions, and how each is dissolved

| ❌ Confusion | ✅ Dissolution |
|---|---|
| **Fascism and socialism are both "collectivist", so they belong together** | The chapter is explicit that fascism **rejects equality and substitutes the principle of hierarchy**, while socialism in **all** its variants pursues equality through public control. They share only a critique of **unregulated** capitalism, and even that is misleading — fascism was **strongly opposed to democracy (if not to capitalism)** |
| **Socialism is just another word for Marxism** | Socialism is the wider family. The chapter's own varieties include **Fabian socialism, revisionism, syndicalism, guild socialism, parliamentary or democratic socialism**; revolutionary socialism alone **belongs to the Marxist tradition**, while evolutionary socialism **belongs to the liberal tradition** |
| **Anarchism means chaos** | **Most anarchists recognize the rational form of authority … as also the moral authority of collective decisions taken in a genuinely democratic manner**. The target is **compulsory** and **hierarchical** authority, not coordination |
| **Gandhi and Marx are basically the same because both want a classless society** | The chapter's own comparison gives eight contrasts; the generative one is that Gandhi locates class in **mental outlook based on contempt for physical labour** while Marx locates it in **the existence of private property** |
| **Conservatism is reaction, or a species of fascism** | **Reaction restores** a superseded order; **conservatism conserves and repairs** the existing one; **fascism reconstructs** around a leader and **overrides inherited constitutional restraint** — which is precisely what a Burkean defends |

### 3. The qualified answer thesis

⚠️ **Adaptable to any directive:** *Socialism, anarchism and Gandhism are three genuinely rival egalitarian responses to unregulated capitalism and coercive power, differing chiefly on how much coercive state authority — if any — is legitimate and on whether change comes by reform, voluntary federation or moral persuasion; fascism is not a fourth member of this family but its explicit negation, replacing equality with hierarchy and liberty with subordination; and conservatism stands outside all four, declining to steer change toward any designed end-state at all.*

### 4. Directive decoding

| Directive | What it demands here | ❌ Trap |
|---|---|---|
| **Define** | Genus plus differentia, then one contrast — e.g. conservatism against reaction, or anarchism against disorder | A sentence of description with no boundary |
| **Distinguish** | Separate the named doctrines on the **state / property / equality** axes of §1 | Treating them as one undifferentiated anti-capitalist or anti-liberal bloc |
| **Compare** | The synthesis table **plus** one named evidence unit per side | Comparing without naming a thinker or a mechanism for each |
| **Critically examine** | Test whether fascism belongs with the other three as an equality-seeking critique — and show why it does not | Grouping fascism with socialism because both criticise unregulated capitalism |
| **Discuss** | Each doctrine's aim, method, view of state and property, one criticism, then a verdict | Omitting anarchism or Gandhism as if only socialism and fascism were on offer |
| **"How far"** | A **graded** verdict, naming the conditions under which the claim holds | An unqualified yes or no |

### 5. Reusable named evidence units

| | Claim | Named | ⚠️ Limitation |
|---|---|---|---|
| **U1** | Democracy can be **redefined** to include control over wealth production, achieved through existing constitutional machinery | **Sydney Webb** (⚠️ standard form **Sidney Webb**), *Fabian Essays* 1889 | Relies on the patience of legislative majorities; the chapter calls Fabianism **an intellectual luxury** |
| **U2** | **Power of the state and power of capital are coterminous**, so the proletariat **cannot emancipate itself by acquiring and using state power** | **Proudhon** | Coterminous is asserted, not argued |
| **U3** | **Sociability** is the greatest advantage in the struggle for life, so government is unnecessary once people are **not corrupted by the state and law** | **Kropotkin**, *Mutual Aid* (1890-96) | The chapter's reductio: if nature were that benign, the ills would not have arisen |
| **U4** | Fascism **came to rescue capitalism from this dilemma** by abolishing democratic rights, unions and free criticism | **Laski**, *State in Theory and Practice* (1935) | Functional, not causal — Ebenstein denies the causal version |
| **U5** | Fascism arises **only where democracy is particularly weak**, and depression supplies **fear and frustration** rather than fascism directly | **Ebenstein**, *Today's Isms* (1980) | Identifies conditions, not sufficient causes |
| **U6** | **Means are after all everything**; realisation of the goal is **in exact proportion to that of the means** | **Gandhi** (Bose, *Selections from Gandhi*, 1948) | A very strong proportionality claim, admitting **no exception** |
| **U7** | Owners should hold possessions **only as 'trustees' … for the service of humanity**, with **sarvodaya** as the social goal | **Gandhi**; sarvodaya ⚠️ cross-chapter, PDF pp.489-490 | The chapter's own objection: **hardly realizable in actual practice** |
| **U8** | 🔶 An order that cannot change **cannot preserve itself**, so reform is the instrument of conservation | **Burke**, *Reflections* (1790) | No stated criterion for when a defect warrants reform |

### 6. Executable architecture

**10 marks (~150 words).** One-line qualified thesis → contrast two doctrines on the **state** axis → one named evidence unit → one-line verdict on why fascism is the outlier.

**15 marks (~200-250 words).** Thesis → compressed premises-mechanism-conclusion spine → two or three evidence units spanning at least two doctrines → one objection-and-reply chain → verdict using the liberty-equality-state-property synthesis.

**20 marks (~250-300 words).** Thesis with directive fidelity → full spine → four or five evidence units spanning socialism, anarchism, Gandhism and fascism's own critique → two objection-and-reply chains with residuals conceded → ⚠️ the conceptual Indian application clearly tagged → a reasoned verdict distinguishing equality-seeking critics of capitalism from fascism's hierarchy-seeking negation. ⚠️ Where conservatism is named, substitute Lesson 23's module for one of the four doctrines and add the designed-end-state discriminator.

### 7. Four standing checks before writing

1. ⚠️ **Provenance check.** Is this from the chapter's Topic 05 range (PDF pp.55-83), from the **common-good** chapter (Sarvodaya, PDF pp.489-490), or from **outside the source entirely** (conservatism, Lesson 23)? Is this quotation the chapter's own or one the chapter carries — Joad, Schumpeter, Ebenstein, MacIver, Dunner, Laski, Almond and Powell, Mussolini, Gandhi?
2. 📰 **Date check.** The Scandinavian and India illustrations, the market-socialism country list, the interwar material and the closing remark that **fascist tendencies are not dead the world over** are all **2009** observations. None is a present-day description.
3. ❌ **Boundary check.** No Indian constitutional provision, Directive Principle, scheme, figure or party label. No caste material. No corporatism as an institutional design — the owner routes it to the democracy and representation file. No contemporary regime or movement named as fascist.
4. ⚠️ **Name check.** Standard forms, where an answer speaks in its own voice: **Sidney Webb**, **Sydney Olivier**, **Georges Sorel**. The chapter's printings differ and are reproduced only where the chapter is being reported.

### 8. The conceptual Indian application, bounded

⚠️ This session holds **no** Indian policy data, scheme names, figures or constitutional provisions. What the topic supplies is a **lens**: trusteeship and sarvodaya pose a yardstick — does a given arrangement centre the **last person** first, and does it rely on **moral and voluntary** participation or on coercive redistribution? ⚠️ That is an analytical question to pair with the candidate's own independently verified Polity, Economy or Social-Justice facts; it is **not** a claim about any scheme's design, funding or outcome, and no such claim is asserted here.

Revision notes:
- Three discriminating questions: **does it seek equality** (yes → socialism/anarchism/Gandhism; hierarchy → fascism; no designed end-state → conservatism); **what is the state for**; **how does change happen**.
- The five standing confusions and their dissolutions — fascism-with-socialism, socialism-as-Marxism, anarchism-as-chaos, Gandhi-as-Marx, conservatism-as-reaction.
- Qualified thesis: three rival egalitarian responses differing on coercive state authority and on the mode of change; fascism as the **negation**; conservatism **outside** all four, declining a designed end-state.
- Directive decoding: Define (genus + differentia + contrast); Distinguish (state/property/equality axes); Compare (table + one unit per side); Critically examine (test fascism's membership); Discuss (aim, method, state, property, criticism, verdict); "How far" (graded verdict with conditions).
- Eight evidence units, each with its limitation: Webb; Proudhon; Kropotkin; Laski; Ebenstein; Gandhi on means; trusteeship/sarvodaya; 🔶 Burke.
- Architecture: 10 marks — thesis, state-axis contrast, one unit, verdict; 15 — add the spine and one chain; 20 — add a second chain, residuals, a tagged application, and the conservatism substitution where named.
- Four checks: **provenance**, **date**, **boundary**, **name**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A stem reads: "Fascism, socialism and anarchism are all reactions against liberal capitalism and may be treated together. Critically examine." What is the most efficient structure, and which concession is unavoidable?

**Model answer:** Restate the claim as testable, concede what is true in it, then defeat it on the axis that matters. ⚠️ The concession is unavoidable and should come early: all three **do** stand against unregulated liberal capitalism. The chapter supplies the common ground — socialism's premise that political liberty and equality **will remain an empty form** without economic reorganisation, anarchism's rejection of **capitalist private property and the state** together, and fascism's own position as a **theory of reaction to democracy, socialism and communism** operating in societies gripped by **crisis and abnormal circumstances**. Grant that, and the proposition has a real basis. ⚠️ Then defeat it on **equality**. The chapter is explicit that fascism **rejects equality and substitutes the principle of hierarchy culminating in a supreme leader or dictator whose will is law**, and that it **exalts the state as an end and reduces individual to the means** — whereas socialism in all variants pursues equality through public control, and socialist anarchism treats **social and economic equality as a necessary condition to secure maximum freedom of all**. ⚠️ Two further points close the case. Fascism's opposition to capitalism is itself qualified in the chapter's own parenthesis — it was **strongly opposed to democracy (if not to capitalism)** — and Laski's finding is that it served **the interests of the capitalist class**, which makes the shared-enemy premise doubtful in fascism's case. And the three differ even among themselves on the **state** axis, so treating them together would erase the topic's real structure: socialism reforms it, anarchism abolishes it, Gandhism moralises it into redundancy, fascism makes it an end in itself. ⚠️ Graded verdict: the proposition holds as a description of what each **opposed** and fails as a description of what each **sought** — and since a doctrine is identified by its aims rather than its enemies, the grouping is misleading.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the four standing checks as optional polish. They guard against the errors an examiner can identify with certainty — a quotation attributed to the chapter when the chapter is only carrying it, a 2009 observation presented as current, an Indian factual claim this session does not hold, and a misspelled name where the chapter's own printing differs from the standard. Those are the cheapest marks in the topic to protect.

# VERIFIED PYQ LINKAGE AND ANSWER APPROACHES

## The ownership position, stated plainly

This topic is supplementary conceptual support. No General Studies paper is printed as a Political Theory paper. The subject README maps this topic, with Topics 03 and 04, to one General Studies I syllabus clause on political philosophies — and marks that mapping **PARTIAL**, noting that chronology, wars and decolonisation remain World History's ownership. That is a conceptual mapping, not an ownership claim over any printed question.

A bounded search pass in this session over reporting of the 2025 and 2026 Civil Services (Main) General Studies papers traced **no** question naming socialism, fascism, anarchism, Gandhism or conservatism as political-theory doctrines. ⚠️ That is a search finding over reporting of those papers, not a line-by-line reading of the official texts, and it is recorded with that limit attached.

Consequently: **no General Studies previous-year question is claimed here**, and none is manufactured. The genuine anchors available are the **verified local Philosophy Optional papers**, used below as a clearly separate application source. ⚠️ They are **not** evidence of what a General Studies paper has asked.

No solved model answer appears below. Each entry gives the printed wording, the directive, the conceptual demand, the lesson that teaches what the answer needs, a concise approach, and a boundary.

⚠️ **Wording note applying to every exact stem in this section.** The stems are reproduced as transcribed in the local ledgers. A sibling topic in this subject established that the local 2018-2025 transcription sometimes closes up a printed space before the question mark, so a space may be present in the official papers where none appears here; where official spacing is not independently confirmed in this pass, the ledger is followed and the uncertainty disclosed. The 2018 stem below carries the printed space as the ledger preserves it. Exact reproductions render typographic quotation marks and apostrophes as straight ASCII; that substitution alters no word and no other punctuation in these stems.

## Verified Philosophy Optional anchors — a separate application source

**2026, Paper II, Section A, Q1(c) [10 marks] (paper code KVMS-B-PHL)**
*"Anarchism is not an independent political ideology in itself, rather it is inspired by liberalism on the one hand and socialism on the other. Analyze."*
- **Directive:** Analyze an embedded thesis — state the dependence claim at full strength, test it, deliver a finding.
- **Apparatus tested:** anarchism's internal range and its relation to the two neighbouring families.
- **Taught in:** Lessons 14-18, with Lesson 16 for the socialist pole and Lesson 18 for the liberal pole.
- **Approach:** this is the stem this topic serves best, and the chapter supplies evidence for **both** halves of the thesis. For the socialist root: socialist anarchism treats **social and economic equality as a necessary condition to secure maximum freedom of all** and rejects **capitalist private property and the state** together. For the liberal root: libertarian anarchism is **the revival of laissez faire individualism**, and the chapter files **Hayek** and **Nozick** here while Topic 03 files the same two under neo-liberalism using the same phrase. The chapter's own range statement is the decisive evidence — anarchists run **from the defenders of extreme form of socialism to the champions of extreme form of individualism**. ⚠️ The finding should then be graded: the thesis is strong on **derivation** and weaker on **identity**, because all schools converge on a proposition neither parent holds — that society **can and should be organized without the coercive authority of the state**.
- **Boundary:** the full architectures of liberalism and of Marxism belong to Topics 03 and 04; use their conclusions, do not reconstruct them.

**2025, Paper II, Section A — no stem owned by this topic**
⚠️ The 2025 paper supplied **no** part whose primary demand is socialism, fascism, anarchism, Gandhism or conservatism as doctrines. The two closest parts are named so that the absence is not misread: **Q2(b) [15]** *Evaluate Marxism as a Political Ideology* — owned by **Topic 04**, which carries it as its own 2025 anchor; and **Q2(a) [20]** on the Gandhi-Ambedkar debate on caste discrimination — owned by the **Philosophy caste owner**, and explicitly outside this chapter's range, since the chapter's treatment of Gandhian equality covers **gender and faith** but not caste.

**2024, Paper II, Section A, Q1(e) [10 marks] (PHKM-B-PHL)**
*Compare socialism and communism as two distinct political ideologies.*
- **Directive:** Compare at 10 marks — axes named, differences stated, a closing position.
- **Apparatus tested:** the evolutionary/revolutionary division and the breadth of the socialist family.
- **Taught in:** Lessons 1, 2 and 7.
- **Approach:** the chapter's own division does the work. Socialism is the wider family, with **Fabian socialism, revisionism, syndicalism, guild socialism, parliamentary or democratic socialism** among its varieties; **evolutionary socialism** admits **an attitude of 'compromise'** and **belongs to the liberal tradition**, while **revolutionary socialism** **makes a direct attack on the prevailing contradictions** and **belongs to the Marxist tradition**. ⚠️ The sharpest discriminator is the harmony axis: evolutionary socialism **subscribes to the theory of harmony or equilibrium**, revolutionary socialism **repudiates** it. Close with Lesson 7's directional criterion — socialism **seeks to tilt the balance in favour of the weaker, exploited, underprivileged and oppressed sections**.
- **Boundary:** the content of communism — historical materialism, surplus value, the classless society — is Topic 04's; take the conclusions, do not rebuild them.

**2023, Paper II, Section A, Q1(b) [10 marks] (SKYC-B-PHLY)**
*Critically examine the anarchist's view that "all States always and everywhere are illegitimate and unjust."*
- **Directive:** critically examine an embedded universal — decode the quantifiers first.
- **Apparatus tested:** the precise target of anarchist objection, and the family's internal disagreement.
- **Taught in:** Lessons 14, 15 and 18.
- **Approach:** attack the quantifiers before the substance. **Always and everywhere** is an absolute, and the chapter supplies counter-instances from **inside** anarchism: **most anarchists recognize the rational form of authority … as also the moral authority of collective decisions taken in a genuinely democratic manner**, and libertarian anarchists **do not recommend to abolish the state altogether**. ⚠️ So the universal is not held by the whole family. What **is** held is narrower and stronger: that **no individual, whether state official or not, has the right to command the obedience of another**, which attacks the command-relation rather than every state. Close with the chapter's own two criticisms from Lesson 18.
- **Boundary:** the concept of legitimacy and the full theory of political obligation are owned elsewhere; use them, do not reconstruct them.

**2022, Paper II, Section A, Q4(b) [15 marks] (CRNA-S-PHLY)**
*Does idea of unconditional rights necessarily lead to anarchy? Critically examine.*
- **Directive:** critically examine a necessity claim — **necessarily** is the word to attack.
- **Apparatus tested:** the distinction between anarchy as a doctrine and anarchy as disorder.
- **Taught in:** Lessons 14, 15 and 18.
- **Approach:** ⚠️ this stem is **primarily owned elsewhere**, by the individual-and-state owner, and this topic contributes one decisive clarification: the word **anarchy** is being used in two senses, and the chapter's own material separates them. Anarchism as a doctrine is an ordered proposal with retained authorities and institutional designs — **mutual credit bank**, **federal principle**, **communes**, **trade unions**. Anarchy as disorder is what Lesson 4's Webb called **the anarchy of competitive struggle**, used pejoratively. ⚠️ Once separated, the necessity claim fails in one sense and becomes an empirical question in the other. Nozick's position supplies the sharpest illustration: rights treated as near-absolute yield not disorder but a minimal state that **would approximate to anarchy**.
- **Boundary:** the theory of rights is owned by the rights and ideals files.

**2021, Paper II, Section A, Q3(a) [20 marks] (HXS-B-PHLY)**
*Discuss anarchism as a political ideology. Is it possible to dispense with political authority completely? Give reasons for your answer.*
- **Directive:** two demands at 20 marks — an exposition and a reasoned position. Both are separately markable.
- **Apparatus tested:** the whole anarchism block.
- **Taught in:** Lessons 14-18.
- **Approach:** exposition first, using the chapter's structure rather than a list — the definition, the one common aim with its two axes of disagreement, the authority ledger, then the six schools in their nested order. For the second demand, the chapter supplies the decisive distinction: **political authority** defined as **the art of persuading others when they do not agree** may be retained even by philosophical anarchists, while **the coercive authority of the state** is what is dispensed with. ⚠️ So the honest answer is that anarchism dispenses with **coercive** authority, not with political authority as such — and the chapter's own Lesson 18 criticisms then supply the grounds for a graded verdict.
- ⚠️ **Note:** 2021 also set **Q3(b) [15]** on **Gandhian Socialism**, which this topic could equally serve. One anchor per year is listed; Q3(b) is named here so the curation is transparent.

**2020, Paper II, Section A, Q1(e) [10 marks] (URC-B-PHLY)**
*Do you think that the prevailing pandemic will lead to anarchism in society? Discuss.*
- **Directive:** Discuss a predictive question — a position is required.
- **Apparatus tested:** the doctrine/disorder distinction under pressure from a topical framing.
- **Taught in:** Lessons 14 and 18.
- **Approach:** ⚠️ the stem almost certainly uses **anarchism** in the popular sense of breakdown, and the single most valuable move is to say so and separate the senses. Anarchism as a doctrine is a **mode of thought** holding that society **can and should be organized without the coercive authority of the state** — crisis does not produce a doctrine. What crisis might produce is disorder, which anarchists do not advocate. ⚠️ The chapter's own Lesson 18 criticism cuts the other way entirely: large-scale problems make **the need of regulation … all the more evident and pressing**, and even state authority is called **inadequate**. So on the chapter's reasoning a crisis strengthens the case for **more** coordinated authority, not less.
- **Boundary:** ❌ no contemporary epidemiological, economic or policy claim is asserted from this session; the answer stays at the level of the conceptual distinction.

**2019, Paper II, Section A, Q1(c) [10 marks] (SDF-B-PHLY)**
*Evaluate Mahatma Gandhi as a political anarchist.*
- **Directive:** Evaluate — name the criterion, test the classification, deliver a graded verdict.
- **Apparatus tested:** the fit between Gandhism and the anarchist family.
- **Taught in:** Lessons 14, 17, 19-22.
- **Approach:** the criterion should be the family's own: **abolition of the state**. Gandhi qualifies on the **end** — the state is **a soulless machine for coercion of individuals** which, when non-violence is **adopted as a universal principle**, **would become redundant**. He qualifies on the **lineage** too, since the chapter itself records that Gandhi **was inspired by** Tolstoy's pacific anarchism **in developing his philosophy of non-violence**. ⚠️ Where he differs is in building an organised collective method — satyagraha — plus a constructive economic and social programme in trusteeship, bread labour and sarvodaya, which Tolstoy's individual moral witness does not supply. ⚠️ Graded verdict: Gandhi satisfies the anarchist criterion on the end-state and departs from the pacific-anarchist model on method and programme — so **a political anarchist by destination, with a constructive programme no anarchist school supplies**.
- **Boundary:** no claim about Gandhi's political career, campaigns or constitutional role is made from this chapter.

**2018, Paper II, Section A, Q2(c) [15 marks] (EGT-D-PHLY)**
*Do you subscribe to the political ideology of Anarchists ? Justify your answer.*
- **Directive:** a first-person position with justification — a verdict is mandatory and must be argued.
- **Apparatus tested:** the whole anarchism block, assessed rather than described.
- **Taught in:** Lessons 14-18.
- **Approach:** a defensible structure is to subscribe to the **diagnosis** and withhold assent from the **programme**, which is exactly where the chapter lands. The diagnosis to endorse is the chapter's own concession: the schools **draw our attention to the tyranny of economic and political power which makes the life of people so miserable**, and anarchism's **significance lies in devising suitable means to curb that power**. The programme to resist is met by the two criticisms — the human-nature reductio, and the scale argument that **worldwide terrorism, crime and environmental pollution** require a **global authority** to **supplement** the state. ⚠️ Strengthen the answer by supplying Kropotkin's reply to the first criticism — the state as **corrupter** rather than remedy — and then conceding that both causal claims are asserted rather than demonstrated.
- **Boundary:** ⚠️ the stem's spacing before the question mark is preserved as the ledger carries it.

## Curation note

⚠️ **Nine year-entries across 2018-2026, supplying eight owned stems.** One entry per year is listed; **2025 is explicitly empty** of a stem this topic owns, and its two closest parts are named with their actual owners. ⚠️ Where a year offered more than one candidate — 2021's Q3(a) on anarchism and Q3(b) on Gandhian Socialism — the second is named in the entry so the selection is transparent.

⚠️ Several stems are **primarily owned elsewhere** and each entry says so. Listing a stem here is a claim about **what this topic contributes to an answer**, not a claim of ownership. ⚠️ What the list demonstrates is a clear pattern: across nine years the Optional examiner returned to **anarchism** six times, which is why this topic's anarchism block is taught across five lessons rather than compressed.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

### Cumulative check A — Lessons 1 to 7 (socialism)

**Question:** Trace the socialism arc from its definitional problem to the chapter's own verdict, naming each step.

**Model answer:** The chapter concedes that socialism is **variously understood** and that many works have **evaded the problem of defining it**, quoting Joad that it is **like a hat that has lost its shape because everybody wears it**; three definitions nonetheless converge on public ownership or control of the major means of production **in the interests of all**, grounded in the claim that political liberty and equality **will remain an empty form** without economic reorganisation (Lesson 1). The varieties then differ by their **answers to the question of method**: evolutionary socialism proceeds **by degrees**, admits **an attitude of 'compromise'** and **belongs to the liberal tradition**, subscribing to **the theory of harmony or equilibrium**; revolutionary socialism seeks socialism **in its totality**, **belongs to the Marxist tradition** and **repudiates** the equilibrium theory (Lesson 2). Fabianism supplies the first systematic evolutionary doctrine, modifying Marxism in economics (**the Ricardian law of rent**), agent (**'permeating' the middle class**) and method (socialism **by degrees**), licensed by Britain's **1865 to 1885** democratic conditions (Lesson 3), with four stated aims and Webb's redefinition of democracy as **control … of the main instruments of wealth production** (Lesson 4). Lassalle keeps the Marxian prediction and changes the route; Bernstein attacks the prediction empirically and concludes that **liberal democracy was … the very substance of socialism** (Lesson 5). Syndicalism and guild socialism relocate the future order in unions and guilds, the latter retaining a successor body for **civic activities** (Lesson 6). The chapter then charges the family with having **no coherent doctrine**, with not being **based in the working class**, and with serving as a **'safety valve'** — before concluding that evolutionary socialism is **an effective instrument for mitigating the rigours of capitalism** that **fails to transform capitalism precisely for want of adequate, organized and consistent public pressure** (Lesson 7).

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the varieties as rival conceptions of socialism's **aim**. The chapter's own organising sentence is that **the varieties of socialism differ from each other because of their different answers to this important question** — the question being **how can socialism be established in society?** They disagree about method, not destination.

### Cumulative check B — Lessons 8 to 13 (fascism)

**Question:** Why does the chapter refuse to treat fascism as a political philosophy, and how does it support that refusal?

**Model answer:** Because it holds that fascism's incoherence is a **predictable consequence of its political strategy**, not an accident. The chapter's claim is that fascism embraced principles **only to win the political support of some groups**, especially to mobilize **frustrated elements**, and that **since these heterogeneous groups had no common interest, no common ideal and no common values, fascism could never evolve a consistent political theory** (Lesson 8). The support is threefold. Mussolini's own 1924 admission that the Fascists were **aristocrats and democrats, revolutionaries and reactionaries, proletarians and anti-proletarians, pacifists and antipacifists**, with **a single fixed point: the nation**, corroborates the analysis from inside. Laski's description of fascism as **an ill-assorted rag-bag** corroborates it from outside. And the Naples speech of 1922 shows the truth-criterion itself abandoned — **it is not necessary that it shall be a reality** (Lesson 12). ⚠️ The chapter therefore directs students to fascism **not for guidance in sorting out theoretical issues, but for understanding 'political pathology'** — how leaders mobilize people for **goals which they would not approve of under normal mental and social conditions**. The substantive content is then set out as a **theory of reaction** (equality replaced by **hierarchy culminating in a supreme leader**, the state as **an end** and the individual as **the means**) and a **theory of counter-revolution** (concentrating economic control while stopping political diffusion, so that capitalism is **stripped … of its democratic character**), closing with the verdict that fascism is **a sick mental and political attitude**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not accept the clubbing of fascism with communist totalitarianism. The chapter calls it **not only unfair but misleading**, on the ground that fascism suppresses **to secure the interests of a tiny class** while communism, **even when it uses coercion**, **seeks to distribute benefits** — a distinction Almond and Powell locate in **distributive capability**. The ruling is about purpose, not about means.

### Cumulative check C — Lessons 14 to 18 (anarchism)

**Question:** Set out the anarchist family's structure, and show how the chapter's own text defeats both the "chaos" caricature and the claim that the family is unified.

**Model answer:** The family has **one common aim: abolition of the state**, with wide difference on **how the state should be abolished** and **what type of organization should be evolved to replace it**, ranging **from the defenders of extreme form of socialism to the champions of extreme form of individualism**. The chaos caricature is defeated by the authority ledger: all reject **compulsory forms of authority, culminating in authority of the state**, and most are averse to **hierarchical** forms — **churches, armies, bosses of capitalist enterprises, and impersonal bureaucracies** — but most **recognize the rational form of authority, particularly the authority of experts** and **the moral authority of collective decisions taken in a genuinely democratic manner**. ⚠️ Retained authority is bounded by **competence** or by **consent**; rejected authority is unbounded by either. The unity claim is defeated by the schools themselves: philosophical anarchism reaches Stirner, who **recognizes no duties to others** and acts **within the limit of his might**; socialist anarchism divides on distribution between Proudhon's reward **only … for his labour** and Kropotkin's **according to needs**; Bakunin's **propaganda by the deed** extends to **acts of assassination and terrorism** while Tolstoy holds the state **tried to fight evil with another evil**; Sorel is judged **not … a full-fledged anarchist**; and libertarians **do not recommend to abolish the state altogether**, which means they do not share the family's defining aim. The chapter's own appraisal then charges the whole family with being **too optimistic** about human nature and **terribly inadequate** at the scale of **worldwide terrorism, crime and environmental pollution**, while conceding that its **significance lies in devising suitable means to curb** the **tyranny of economic and political power**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the libertarian section as showing that anarchism and minimal-state liberalism are the same. The chapter's own sentence settles it: **libertarians stand for restricting the role of the state to minimum possible level. They do not recommend to abolish the state altogether.** The placement is the chapter's; the mismatch with the family's defining aim is a tension it does not resolve.

### Cumulative check D — Lessons 19 to 22 (Gandhism)

**Question:** Show how Gandhism's economics follows from its ethics rather than sitting beside it.

**Model answer:** The chain runs from ahimsa to trusteeship in four steps. First, ahimsa is defined positively, not merely as **abstention from violence** but as **love of all**, so that **it is non-violence only when we love those that hate us**. Second, the concept of violence is extended, and the decisive extension is economic: **acquiring material things beyond one's immediate need is also a form of violence because thereby we deprive others of their share** (Lesson 20). Third, that makes accumulation beyond need an **ongoing injury**, so the remedy is not charity but the cessation of a wrong — which is exactly what trusteeship demands, urging owners **not to consider themselves as the sole proprietors of their possessions, but only as 'trustees' of a gift bestowed upon them by God for the service of humanity** (Lesson 21). Fourth, the mechanism is supplied by the same ethics: Gandhi **relied on the power of truth and non-violence (ahimsa) to accomplish 'change of heart'**, and Lesson 20 had already established that non-violence is **the power of the strong** which **results in the 'change of heart' of the mighty opponent**. ⚠️ The same derivation explains the rest: class is located in **mental outlook based on contempt for physical labour**, so the remedy is **bread labour** producing **dignity of labour** and then equality **transcending the prevailing division of labour**; and sarvodaya names the goal — because it **believes in uplift of all** it **does not envisage a conflict between the high and the low, between the rich and the poor**, while it **gives prominence to the uplift of the deprived and the underprivileged sections**. ⚠️ The chapter's own objection must close the answer: the change of heart **is a fascinating idea, but it is hardly realizable in actual practice**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read Gandhi's preference for simple technology as mere nostalgia. The chapter gives reasons — simple technology has **the capacity of mass employment** while advanced technology **would create vast unemployment, particularly in a country like India, and would promote consumerism with all its ill effects** — and records the realism that it was **not feasible to switch over to the new system abruptly**, with the existing system **allowed to continue with necessary changes in the attitude of the owners**.

### Cumulative check E — the whole topic

**Question:** You have two minutes before a 20-mark answer naming three of these doctrines. List the checks you run, in order, and what each prevents.

**Model answer:** First, run the **three discriminating questions** — does the doctrine seek equality, seek hierarchy, or decline a designed end-state; what is the state for; how does change happen — and write the qualified thesis from the answers. This prevents an opening that lists features. Second, fix which doctrines the question actually names and resist importing the others; this prevents a survey. Third, select **evidence units** with their limitations attached — Webb, Proudhon, Kropotkin, Laski, Ebenstein, Gandhi on means, trusteeship-and-sarvodaya, and Burke where conservatism is named; this prevents name-dropping. Fourth, choose an **objection-and-reply chain** and concede its residual; the strongest pairings are the trusteeship change-of-heart objection, the anarchist human-nature reductio with Kropotkin's reply, and the Laski/Ebenstein functional-versus-causal dispute. Fifth, run the **provenance check**: is this the chapter's Topic 05 range, the common-good chapter for sarvodaya, or outside the source entirely for conservatism; and is this quotation the chapter's own or one it carries. Sixth, run the **date check**: the Scandinavia and India illustrations, the market-socialism country list, the interwar material and the closing remark on fascist tendencies are all 2009 observations. Seventh, run the **boundary check**: no Indian provision, scheme, figure or party label; no caste material; no corporatism as institutional design; no contemporary regime named as fascist. Eighth, run the **name check** — Sidney Webb, Sydney Olivier, Georges Sorel in one's own voice. Then write the verdict: equality-seeking critics against hierarchy-seeking fascism, with conservatism outside both.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the four standing checks as decoration. They protect against the only errors an examiner can establish with certainty — a misattributed quotation, a 2009 observation presented as current, an imported factual claim, and a misspelled name — which makes them the highest-value seconds available.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ The questions below are original practice prompts written for this session. They are not previous-year questions and must not be cited as such.

### 10-mark practice (~150 words)

**Practice question 1 [10]:** Distinguish evolutionary socialism from revolutionary socialism. *(Distinguish — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** Evolutionary socialism is achieved by degrees rather than by wholesale transformation in a single stroke; it admits an attitude of compromise between capitalism and socialism, allowing the capitalist system to continue with changes in the socialist direction, and therefore belongs to the liberal tradition. It relies on the democratic method, parliamentary reform and even economic planning, and is coterminous with democratic socialism. Revolutionary socialism seeks socialism in its totality, makes a direct attack on the prevailing contradictions of the social order, and belongs to the Marxist tradition; it organises the working classes to overthrow the capitalist order by revolution. ⚠️ The deepest difference is theoretical rather than tactical: evolutionary socialism subscribes to the theory of harmony or equilibrium as the governing principle of social relationships, while revolutionary socialism repudiates reconciliation between interests and seeks ultimately to destroy the conditions of domination itself.

**Scoring note for this answer:** marks concentrate in the final sentence, because it moves the contrast from **pace** to **theory** — whether class interests are reconcilable in principle. The tradition assignment is the second gain: stating that one branch belongs to the liberal tradition is a precise, source-grounded classification that most answers do not attempt.

**Practice question 2 [10]:** Why does the chapter refuse to treat anarchism as a doctrine of chaos? *(Why — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** Because anarchism's target is coercion, not coordination, and the chapter sets out exactly which authorities survive. All anarchists reject compulsory forms of authority culminating in the authority of the state, and most are averse to hierarchical authority — churches, armies, bosses of capitalist enterprises, impersonal bureaucracies. But most recognise the rational authority of experts within their fields of competence, and the moral authority of collective decisions taken in a genuinely democratic manner. ⚠️ The retained authorities are principled rather than residual: one is bounded by competence, the other by consent, and what anarchism rejects is authority unbounded by either. The definition itself affirms organisation, holding that society can and should be organised without the coercive authority of the state. ⚠️ Philosophical anarchists go further and accept political authority understood as the art of persuading others when they do not agree.

**Scoring note for this answer:** a "why" question is failed by description, so the answer gives the principle — competence or consent — that makes the retained authorities consistent with the rejection. The closing sentence earns the extra mark by showing that even the most radical school retains something, which is harder evidence than the general claim.

### 15-mark practice (~200-250 words)

**Practice question 3 [15]:** Examine the claim that fascism is a species of socialism because both criticise unregulated capitalism. *(Examine — answer in 200 to 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** Restated as testable, the claim is that a shared critique of laissez-faire capitalism places the two doctrines in one family. There is a little support for it. Fascism began as a movement demanding revolutionary changes in Italy's social and political structure, its *fasci* were of workers, and it did attack the liberal order that socialism also attacked.

The counter-evidence is decisive. First, the chapter is explicit that fascism **rejects equality and substitutes the principle of hierarchy culminating in a supreme leader or dictator whose will is law**, while socialism in all its variants pursues equality through public control — the two stand for opposite social values. Second, fascism's opposition to capitalism is doubtful on the chapter's own text: it was **strongly opposed to democracy (if not to capitalism)**, and Laski concludes that, stripped of rhetoric, Italian fascism was **an insistence upon compulsory obedience to a state whose purpose is to protect existing class-relations**. Third, the counter-revolution analysis shows fascism concentrating economic control and stopping the diffusion of political power, producing **lower wages**, **lowering of taxation upon capital** and **contraction of social services** — the opposite of socialism's direction.

Attacking the logic: a shared enemy does not establish a shared aim, and doctrines are identified by what they seek.

⚠️ Finding: the claim fails. Fascism and socialism share a target and nothing else; indeed the chapter records that **the capitalists and the communists joined together** to defeat fascism.

**Scoring note for this answer:** "Examine" requires the claim restated as testable, evidence both ways, and a finding, which the structure supplies. The decisive move is attacking the premise rather than the conclusion — showing that fascism's anti-capitalism is itself doubtful on the source's own text, which removes the shared ground the claim depends on.

**Practice question 4 [15]:** "Gandhian trusteeship is a fascinating idea with no mechanism." Critically examine. *(Critically examine — answer in 200 to 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The proposition is close to the chapter's own, which records that critics find the vision of a change of heart of the rich **a fascinating idea, but … hardly realizable in actual practice**. Its force is real: trusteeship asks landlords and capitalists to regard themselves **only as 'trustees' of a gift bestowed upon them by God**, and compliance depends on their disposition rather than on any institution.

Three qualifications follow. First, a mechanism **is** supplied: Gandhi relied on **the power of truth and non-violence (ahimsa) to accomplish 'change of heart'**, and non-violence is described as **the power of the strong** which **results in the 'change of heart' of the mighty opponent and makes him bend** — so the objection must be about the method's **reach**, not its absence. Second, an incentive is offered: trustees acting as public servants would win **wide public respect instead of the existing hatred**. Third, the demand rests on a prior claim that makes it more than an appeal — **acquiring material things beyond one's immediate need is also a form of violence**, so the trustee is ceasing to do harm rather than being generous.

Attacking the logic: "no mechanism" is an absolute, and a single supplied mechanism defeats it.

⚠️ Verdict: the proposition fails as stated and succeeds in a narrower form — the mechanism exists but is **non-institutional**, so non-compliance carries no sanction, which is the real vulnerability.

**Scoring note for this answer:** the critical element is discharged by defeating an absolute with a counter-instance and then conceding the sharper version of the objection. Narrowing an objection rather than refuting it outright is the move that distinguishes a critical answer from a defensive one.

### 20-mark practice (~250-300 words)

**Practice question 5 [20]:** Critically evaluate anarchism as a political doctrine. *(Critically evaluate — answer in 250 to 300 words.)*

**Model answer:** Evaluation needs a criterion. The one adopted here is whether the doctrine identifies a real pathology and supplies a remedy proportionate to it.

On the first half anarchism succeeds. Its target is precise — not order but **compulsory forms of authority, culminating in authority of the state**, and hierarchical authority in **churches, armies, bosses of capitalist enterprises, and impersonal bureaucracies** — while rational and moral authority survive. Proudhon identifies the pathology sharply: **power of the state and power of capital are coterminous**, so **the proletariat cannot emancipate itself by acquiring and using state power**. The chapter itself concedes the point, recording that the schools **draw our attention to the tyranny of economic and political power**.

On the second half it is weaker, on two independent grounds. The chapter's reductio: anarchism **takes a too optimistic view of human nature**, and if nature were **so benign**, the ills it wants to remedy **would not have arisen at all**. And the scale argument: **worldwide terrorism, crime and environmental pollution** make regulation **more evident and pressing**, with even state authority **inadequate** and a **global authority** needed — so anarchism's decentralising direction runs the wrong way.

The first criticism has a reply. Kropotkin holds that people **not corrupted by the state and law** would develop **bonds of instinctive solidarity**, which reconciles benign nature with present ills by identifying the state as corrupter. ⚠️ Both causal claims are asserted rather than demonstrated, so the exchange ends in a standoff.

⚠️ Graded verdict: anarchism meets the criterion as diagnosis and fails it as programme — its **significance lies in devising suitable means to curb that power**, which is where the chapter itself locates its value.

**Scoring note for this answer:** "Critically evaluate" is failed by an unnamed criterion, so it is declared first and every judgment refers back to it. The move that lifts this is supplying Kropotkin's reply to the chapter's own reductio and then conceding that both sides assert rather than demonstrate — which converts a one-sided criticism into a genuine objection-reply chain with a residual.

**Practice question 6 [20]:** "Conservatism and Gandhism both appeal to inherited practice, and are therefore varieties of traditionalism." Discuss. *(Discuss — answer in 250 to 300 words.)*

**Model answer:** The proposition has a surface plausibility that must be stated before it is tested. 🔶 Conservatism treats inherited institutions as a store of accumulated practical experience and places the burden of proof on those proposing redesign. Gandhi preferred simple technology over heavy industry, production by the masses over mass production, and grounded his ethics in the moral teachings of religion. Both resist imported blueprints.

The proposition fails on three grounds. First, **direction**: Burkean conservatism has **no designed end-state**, its goal being the continued working of the existing order, repaired; Gandhism pursues a radical designed transformation — swaraj, a classless society, sarvodaya's **uplift of all**. Second, **inherited hierarchy**: conservatism grants it a presumption until it demonstrably fails, while Gandhi attacks it directly, locating class in **mental outlook based on contempt for physical labour** and prescribing compulsory bread labour to destroy that outlook. Third, **property**: conservatism treats property as a stake in the order, whereas trusteeship subordinates it, since **acquiring material things beyond one's immediate need is also a form of violence**.

Attacking the logic: the inference moves from a shared **source** of appeal to a shared **doctrine**, which does not follow — appealing to indigenous practice against imported models is not the same as granting inherited arrangements a presumption of legitimacy.

⚠️ Verdict: the proposition holds only as an observation about rhetorical register and fails as a classification. The true discriminator across all three routes to change is whether reform is steered toward a designed end-state: Fabianism is gradualism with a blueprint, Burkean reform is gradualism because it distrusts blueprints, and Gandhism has the most restrained means of the three with the most radical ends.

**Scoring note for this answer:** a Discuss stem requires both sides fairly stated and a verdict; the distinguishing move is the logical attack — naming the inference from shared source to shared doctrine as a non-sequitur — and then closing on the three-route discriminator, which shows the comparison has been placed within the module rather than answered in isolation.

# REMEDIATION

Each entry names an error, the correction, and where the correction is taught.

| # | Error | Correction | Taught in |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Treating socialism as a synonym for Marxism | Marxism is one variety within a family that also contains **Fabian socialism, revisionism, syndicalism, guild socialism, parliamentary or democratic socialism**; the family is defined by public ownership or control in the interests of all, not by Marx's method | L1, L2 |
| 2 | Thinking the varieties disagree about socialism's goal | They differ because of **their different answers to this important question** — **how can socialism be established in society?** The disagreement is about method | L2 |
| 3 | Treating the dictionary definition as the chapter's own position | The chapter sets out three definitions — Joad's diagnosis, the **Oxford English Dictionary** minimal form and **Schumpeter**'s institutional test — before stating its own working formulation; they are not interchangeable | L1 |
| 4 | Citing the early nineteenth-century socialists to this chapter | ❌ Its socialism section does **not** treat them; Owen, Fourier and Saint-Simon are not named anywhere in it, and no "utopian socialism" material may be anchored to this page range | L1, L2 |
| 5 | Mis-stating what Fabian economics replaced | The chapter's own wording is that the Fabians **based their economics on the Ricardian law of rent rather than on the labour theory of value**. ⚠️ The displaced doctrine is the **labour theory of value**, not the theory of surplus value | L3 |
| 6 | Quoting the Fabian motto as a counsel of delay only | It has two limbs — wait **for the right moment**, then **strike hard** — and the chapter glosses the second as deriving from the **Quintus Fabius** precedent | L3 |
| 7 | Reading Fabian gradualism as merely slow | Webb's own terms are **democratic**, **gradual**, **not regarded as immoral** and **constitutional and peaceful**; the method is procedural, not merely unhurried | L3, L4 |
| 8 | Treating "compromise" as a critic's accusation against evolutionary socialism | It is the chapter's own characterisation of the doctrine — it **admits an attitude of 'compromise'—compromise between capitalism and socialism** | L2 |
| 9 | Missing Webb's redefinition of democracy | Webb extends democracy to economic life: **the control … of the main instruments of wealth production** as against **the anarchy of competitive struggle** | L4 |
| 10 | Confusing Lassalle's and Bernstein's revisions | Lassalle retains the Marxian prediction and changes only the route: the working class should **organize itself into a political party with a view to securing universal, equal and direct suffrage so as to make its power legally effective**, giving **a constitutional and peaceful transition from the capitalist state to a workers' state**. Bernstein attacks the prediction itself and concludes **liberal democracy was … the very substance of socialism** | L5 |
| 11 | Forgetting that revisionism had an orthodox opponent, or inventing his argument | The chapter gives **Kautsky** one sentence: he **continued to defend Marxian theories which remained the official doctrine of communist parties of Europe, but revisionist tactics became part of the socialist struggle**. ❌ No doctrine and no works are supplied; do not import the controversy's content | L5 |
| 12 | Thinking syndicalism and guild socialism are the same | Syndicalism stood for **'socialization without state'**; guild socialism **sought to modify Syndicalism by introducing the importance of consumer side by side with the worker**, and having **sought to abolish the old state** it **insisted that a new organization must be evolved to take charge of the many civic activities necessary to the life of the community** | L6 |
| 13 | Ignoring that the chapter delivers a mixed verdict on evolutionary socialism | It is **an effective instrument for mitigating the rigours of capitalism** which **fails to transform capitalism precisely for want of adequate, organized and consistent public pressure** | L7 |
| 14 | Dating the welfare-state and market-socialism illustrations to the present | ⚠️ Scandinavia, India's policy direction and the market-socialism country list are **2009** observations in a Fifth Edition text | L7, L13 |
| 15 | Treating fascism's incoherence as sloppiness | It is presented as a consequence of strategy — principles embraced **only to win the political support of some groups**, which with **no common interest, no common ideal and no common values** made a consistent theory impossible | L8 |
| 16 | Flattening the chapter's four degrees of attribution | It **only developed into a movement which achieved temporary success in Italy**; it was **partly adopted by Adolf Hitler (1889-1945) and the Nazis in Germany, Franco (1892-1975) and the Falangists in Spain**; it **had a marginal following in Britain, France and other European countries**; and **some writers try to find its parallels in Asia, particularly in Japan, and in some Latin American countries, particularly in Argentina**. ❌ Portugal and Salazar are not named in the chapter. ⚠️ Ebenstein, quoted at Lesson 13, asserts more flatly that **Japan became fascist in the 1930s** | L9, L13 |
| 17 | Reading fascism's liberty claim as ordinary liberty | It is inverted: in Dunner's definition, **true liberty, consequently, is found only in subjection to state authority**, and the fascist state requires **complete conformity, rigid discipline, and unquestioning obedience** | L10 |
| 18 | Missing the end/means inversion | **Instead of regarding individual as an end and the state as a means, fascism exalts the state as an end and reduces individual to the means** — the reversal of the liberal relation | L10 |
| 19 | Treating fascist "unity" as ordinary national solidarity | Fascism **repudiates the progressive doctrine of human equality** and **seeks unity through homogeneity**; in Germany **this attitude was embodied in racist doctrines and the programme of liquidation of 'non-Aryan' elements**. ⚠️ Equality regulates difference; homogeneity eliminates it | L10 |
| 20 | Stating the counter-revolution thesis with only one limb | It requires both: concentrating economic control **and** stopping the diffusion of political power, which **stripped capitalism of its democratic character** | L11 |
| 21 | Confusing Laski's and Ebenstein's claims | Laski's is a **functional** claim — fascism served **to protect existing class-relations**; Ebenstein's is **causal** — **the conditions of capitalism do not by themselves give rise to fascism, but it arises only where democracy is particularly weak**, since **Industrialists are not, as a class, any more fascist-minded than other social groups** | L11, L13 |
| 22 | Reducing fascist leadership to a business conspiracy, or denying the link outright | ❌ The chapter asserts neither. What it supplies is institutional: **the whole state apparatus was used to advance the interests of capitalism at the expense of the worker**, alongside Ebenstein's causal denial that capitalism by itself produces fascism. Do not go beyond those two findings from this chapter | L11, L13 |
| 23 | Treating political myth as a fascist invention | The chapter prints both uses without linking them: fascism **eulogizes the creative power of the myth to muster the support of all classes in society**, and Sorel **recommended the use of 'myth' to mobilize masses into action**. ⚠️ The convergence is real and worth naming; a **borrowing** is not asserted by the chapter and should not be claimed | L12, L17 |
| 24 | Missing why the Naples speech matters | It abandons the truth-criterion itself — **it is not necessary that it shall be a reality** — which is what makes a political philosophy impossible | L12 |
| 25 | Accepting the fascism/communism equation | The chapter calls it **not only unfair but misleading**: fascism suppresses **to secure the interests of a tiny class**, communism **seeks to distribute benefits**, with Almond and Powell locating the difference in **distributive capability** | L13 |
| 26 | Equating anarchism with chaos | Anarchism holds that society **can and should be organized without the coercive authority of the state**; order without coercion is the proposal | L14 |
| 27 | Mis-stating the school list | The chapter names six: **philosophical anarchism, socialist anarchism, revolutionary anarchism, anarcho-syndicalism, pacific anarchism, and libertarian anarchism**. ⚠️ **'Individualist anarchism'** is its second name for the first school, **because of its focus on individual** — not a seventh school, and no "egoistic" school is named | L14, L15 |
| 28 | Treating Stirner as representative of anarchism | He sits inside philosophical/individualist anarchism, at its limit: **every individual is the unique one who truly 'owns himself'**; he **recognizes no duties to others, and does what is right for himself, within the limit of his might** | L15 |
| 29 | Collapsing Proudhon's and Kropotkin's distributive rules | Proudhon: reward **only … for his labour**; Kropotkin: **according to needs**. They differ on the standard, not merely on emphasis | L16 |
| 30 | Importing Proudhon's famous slogan into this chapter | ❌ The chapter never prints it. What it prints is the proposition itself: **each person might possess his means of production (tools, land, etc.) either singly or collectively, but should only be rewarded for his labour**, a system that **will eliminate the elements of profit and rent**. ⚠️ So the critique targets **reward without labour**, not possession, which is why the **mutual credit bank** lends at **a minimal rate of interest, covering only its cost of administration** | L16 |
| 31 | Treating Bakunin's methods as the anarchist norm | **Propaganda by the deed** extending to **acts of assassination and terrorism** is Bakunin's; Tolstoy's pacific anarchism holds the state **tried to fight evil with another evil** | L17 |
| 32 | Calling Sorel an anarchist without qualification | The chapter places **anarcho-syndicalism** as **another version of revolutionary anarchism** with Sorel as **its chief exponent**, yet rules that **since Sorel relied on organized groups to overthrow capitalist state, and provide for its alternative, he is not regarded a full-fledged anarchist** | L17 |
| 33 | Assuming libertarian anarchists want the state abolished | They **stand for restricting the role of the state to minimum possible level. They do not recommend to abolish the state altogether** — ⚠️ a tension with the family's defining aim that the chapter does not resolve | L18 |
| 34 | Dismissing the human-nature criticism without the reply | Kropotkin's reply is that people **not corrupted by the state and law** would develop **bonds of instinctive solidarity** — the state as corrupter, not remedy. ⚠️ Both causal claims are asserted, not demonstrated | L18 |
| 35 | Forgetting the chapter's concession to anarchism | Its **significance lies in devising suitable means to curb** the **tyranny of economic and political power which makes the life of people so miserable** | L18 |
| 36 | Treating Gandhian means-ends as a moral preference | It is an inseparability claim — **Means and ends are the two sides of a coin. They cannot be separated.** — so **as the means so the end**, and impure means cannot produce a pure end | L19 |
| 37 | Reading ahimsa as abstention only | The definition is positive: **love of all**, and **it is non-violence only when we love those that hate us**; non-violence is **the power of the strong** | L20 |
| 38 | Missing the economic extension of violence | **Acquiring material things beyond one's immediate need is also a form of violence because thereby we deprive others of their share** — this is what makes accumulation an ongoing injury | L20, L21 |
| 39 | Treating trusteeship as philanthropy | Owners are asked to regard themselves **only as 'trustees' of a gift bestowed upon them by God for the service of humanity**; the chapter's own objection is that the change of heart is **a fascinating idea, but … hardly realizable in actual practice** | L21 |
| 40 | Missing bread labour's mechanism | Class is located in **mental outlook based on contempt for physical labour**; compulsory physical labour creates **dignity of labour** and equality **transcending the prevailing division of labour** | L21 |
| 41 | Treating sarvodaya as "the greatest good of all" without its structure | It is **uplift of all**, and because it believes in that it **does not envisage a conflict between the high and the low, between the rich and the poor**, while it **gives prominence to the uplift of the deprived and the underprivileged sections**. ⚠️ Chapter-of-record for the detail is the common-good chapter | L21 |
| 42 | Reducing Gandhi-versus-Marx to violence | They disagree on the unit of analysis (moral individual versus class), on the engine (conversion versus struggle), on technology, and on the state's trajectory — ⚠️ though both envisage a stateless destination | L22 |
| 43 | Treating Gandhi's technology preference as nostalgia | Reasons are given — **capacity of mass employment** against **vast unemployment, particularly in a country like India** and **consumerism with all its ill effects** | L21, L22 |
| 44 | Equating conservatism with reaction or with fascism | 🔶 Conservatism defends an existing order through prudential reform; the theory of reaction **substitutes the principle of hierarchy culminating in a supreme leader**. Only the latter is fascism's | L23, L24 |
| 45 | Calling Hayek a conservative | 🔶 Hayek expressly disowned the label; the chapter files him and Nozick under **libertarian anarchism**, Topic 03 files them under neo-liberalism | L18, L23 |
| 46 | Importing caste into Gandhian equality from this chapter | ❌ The chapter's treatment covers **gender** and **faith**; caste is owned by the Philosophy caste file and must be sourced there | L21, L24 |
| 47 | Reproducing the chapter's name forms in one's own voice | ⚠️ Standard forms are **Sidney Webb**, **Sydney Olivier**, **Georges Sorel**; the chapter prints *Sydney Webb*, both spellings of Olivier, and *George Sorel* | L3, L17 |
| 48 | Citing Stirner's work by the chapter's date | ⚠️ The chapter gives *The Ego and his Own* (**1845**); the standard reference is *Der Einzige und sein Eigentum* (**1844**), usually rendered *The Ego and Its Own* | L15 |
| 49 | Presenting the chapter's closing remark on fascist tendencies as a current claim | ⚠️ It is a **2009** observation, and no contemporary state or movement is named as fascist in this session | L13, L24 |
| 50 | Naming corporatism as fascism's institutional design from this topic | ❌ The owners route corporatism to the democracy file; this topic carries the state-as-end doctrine, not the corporate-state machinery | L10, L24 |

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Map 1 — The five doctrines on the three discriminating questions

| Doctrine | Social aim | What the state is for | Engine of change | Property | Verdict in the chapter |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Socialism (evolutionary)** | Equality through public ownership or control **in the interests of all** | An instrument of social reconstruction, extended by **control … of the main instruments of wealth production** | Democratic, gradual, constitutional and peaceful pressure **by degrees** | Socialised in major means of production; private property curtailed, not abolished | **Effective instrument for mitigating the rigours of capitalism** that **fails to transform** it |
| **Socialism (revolutionary)** | Equality through socialism **in its totality** | To be captured, then superseded | **Direct attack on the prevailing contradictions**; capture of power | Abolished as a class relation | Treated as the Marxist tradition — ➡️ Topic 04 |
| **Fascism** | **Rejects equality**; **hierarchy culminating in a supreme leader** | **An end in itself**, with the individual as **the means** | Mobilisation of **frustrated elements** by myth, coercion and war | Retained; economic control concentrated | **A sick mental and political attitude**; not a political philosophy |
| **Anarchism** | Abolition of the state; equality in socialist strands, liberty in individualist strands | Nothing — **coercive authority** is the target; rational and moral authority survive | Varies by school: mutualism, revolution, **propaganda by the deed**, moral refusal | Proudhon: reward **for his labour**; Kropotkin: **according to needs**; libertarians: protected | **Too optimistic** on human nature and **terribly inadequate** at scale; **significance lies in devising suitable means to curb** power |
| **Gandhism** | **Uplift of all** with **prominence to the uplift of the deprived** | A **soulless machine for coercion**; **redundant** when non-violence is universal | **Change of heart** through truth and non-violence; satyagraha | Trusteeship — owners as **'trustees' of a gift bestowed upon them by God** | Change of heart **hardly realizable in actual practice** |
| 🔶 **Conservatism** | **No designed end-state**; the continued working of the existing order | A custodian of inherited arrangements; the burden of proof lies on the redesigner | Prudential, piecemeal reform **in order to conserve** | A stake in the order, with duties attached | 🔶 Reconstructed from the owner's module; not anchored in this chapter |

⚠️ The verdict column reproduces **the chapter's** assessments, not this session's.

```
   "revisionist tactics became part of the socialist struggle"
   "liberal democracy was ... the very substance of socialism"
         |
   LASSALLE: route changed, prediction kept
   universal, equal and direct suffrage -> workers' party
   -> "constitutional and peaceful transition from the
      capitalist state to a workers' state"
```
*Caption: the family tree is organised by answers to "how?", which is why Lassalle and Bernstein sit on different branches despite both revising Marx.*
