# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

⚠️ Bold here marks emphasis and key terms. Phrases carried from the source are reproduced as taught in the lessons; the lesson bodies and the blockquotes remain the place where exact source wording is presented as such.

## 1. The definitional problem and the three definitions

- Socialism is **variously understood**; many works have **evaded the problem of defining it**. Joad: like **a hat that has lost its shape because everybody wears it**.
- The three definitions the chapter actually sets out: **(1)** the **Oxford English Dictionary** minimal form — **a theory or policy that aims at or advocates the ownership or control of the means of production—capital, land, property, etc.—by the community as a whole and their administration in the interests of all**, which the chapter calls **not very comprehensive** but indicative of **the chief method and goal**; **(2)** **Schumpeter**, *Capitalism, Socialism and Democracy* (1942) — **that organization of society in which the means of production are controlled, and the decisions on how and what to produce and on who is to get what, are made by public authority instead of by privately-owned and privately-managed firms**; **(3)** the chapter's own working formulation — **the major instruments of social production … are placed under the ownership and control of public authority in order to ensure that they are properly utilized to secure the public interest**.
- Grounding claim: political liberty and equality **will remain an empty form** without the reorganisation of economic life.
- ❌ The chapter's socialism section does **not** treat the early nineteenth-century socialists — Owen, Fourier and Saint-Simon are not named in it — so no "utopian socialism" material may be cited to this page range.

## 2. The method question and the two branches

- Organising sentence: **the varieties of socialism differ from each other because of their different answers to this important question** — how can socialism be established in society?
- **Evolutionary socialism**: **by degrees**; it **admits an attitude of 'compromise'—compromise between capitalism and socialism**; **belongs to the liberal tradition**; subscribes to **the theory of harmony or equilibrium**; coterminous with democratic socialism; uses parliamentary reform and planning.
- **Revolutionary socialism**: socialism **in its totality**; **direct attack on the prevailing contradictions**; **belongs to the Marxist tradition**; **repudiates** the harmony theory. ➡️ Content owned by Topic 04.
- Named varieties in the family: **Fabian socialism, revisionism, syndicalism, guild socialism, parliamentary or democratic socialism**.

## 3. Fabianism

- Fabian Society, **1884**, London. Name from **Quintus Fabius** (**275-203 B.C.**), ⚠️ chapter's dates; the standard reference often gives c.280 B.C.
- Motto, two limbs: wait **for the right moment**, then **strike hard**.
- Early figures include **Sydney Webb** and Beatrice Webb, **Sidney/Sydney Olivier**, Graham Wallas, G.B. Shaw. ⚠️ The chapter prints both spellings of Olivier's first name; standard forms are **Sidney Webb (1859-1947)** and **Sydney Olivier (1859-1943)** — effectively swapped against the chapter.
- Three modifications of Marxism, in the chapter's own order: **(a)** they **based their economics on the Ricardian law of rent rather than on the labour theory of value**; **(b)** they **did not rely exclusively on the working class** but took up the task of **'permeating' the middle class with the socialist message**; **(c)** they sought to introduce socialism **not in a single stroke, but by degrees**, through **gradual democratization of society in the political, economic and intellectual fields**.
- Enabling conditions named for Britain **1865 to 1885**: the working classes had **obtained the franchise (right-to-vote)** and **the legalization of trade unions**, and **their influence on legislation and the wage-contract was visibly on the increase** — so **there was no need of a revolution to create a new political mechanism, for it was already in existence and needed only to be used**. ❌ No press or municipal condition is named.
- Webb's four characteristics of the method: **democratic**, **gradual**, **not regarded as immoral**, **constitutional and peaceful**.
- Four aims, under the chapter's own headings: **Emancipation of Land and Industrial Capital** — **the emancipation of land and industrial capital from individual and class ownership, and vesting them in the community for the general benefit**; **Equality of Opportunity** — **rent and interest would be added to the reward of labour**, so **the idle class, living on the labour of others, would necessarily disappear**; **Dissemination of Socialist Ideas** — including **the establishment of equal citizenship for men and women**, by **a slow and gradual turning of the popular mind**; **Universal Education** — Olivier's insistence on education as **an essential means of emancipation of the working class**.
- Webb's extension of democracy: **the control … of the main instruments of wealth production**, set against **the anarchy of competitive struggle**.

## 4. Lassalle, Bernstein, Kautsky

- **Lassalle**: accepts the Marxian **doctrine of economic interpretation of history** so far as it implies the rise of the working class and the decline of capitalism; **evolved a different view regarding the nature and role of the state**. Instead of violent overthrow, the working class should **organize itself into a political party with a view to securing universal, equal and direct suffrage so as to make its power legally effective** — a **constitutional and peaceful transition from the capitalist state to a workers' state**, with the immediate goal redefined as **securing a majority in the democratic legislative organs of the state**. ❌ The chapter attributes no producers'-association programme to him.
- **Bernstein** (revisionism): attacks the prediction empirically — the middle class had not disappeared and conditions had improved; concludes that **liberal democracy was … the very substance of socialism** and that the movement mattered more than the final goal.
- **Kautsky**: one sentence only — he **continued to defend Marxian theories which remained the official doctrine of communist parties of Europe, but revisionist tactics became part of the socialist struggle**. ⚠️ A split outcome: doctrine to Kautsky, practice to Bernstein. ❌ The chapter gives him no doctrine and no works.

## 5. Syndicalism and guild socialism

- **Syndicalism**: the union is the unit of the future order; the method is the **general strike**; the state is replaced by a **federation of trade unions**. **Sorel** supplies the **myth** of the general strike as a mobilising device.
- **Guild socialism**: it **upheld the Marxian emphasis on class struggle**, **stood for the abolition of the wage system and demanded representation of the workers in industrial control**, and **sought to modify Syndicalism by introducing the importance of consumer side by side with the worker**; having **sought to abolish the old state which was an instrument of exploitation**, it **insisted that a new organization must be evolved to take charge of the many civic activities necessary to the life of the community**.
- ⚠️ Both are counted inside the evolutionary branch by the chapter's roster, though the general strike sits awkwardly with the constitutional-and-peaceful criterion.

## 6. The appraisal of evolutionary socialism

- Three charges: **no coherent doctrine**; not **based in the working class**; functions as a **'safety valve'** for capitalism.
- ⚠️ Session inference, not the chapter's wording: the mitigation defence is usually filled out with welfare provision, labour protection and public services. The chapter itself records only that evolutionary socialism **is practised in a large number of countries today**, bringing **widespread security and prosperity** in Scandinavia and **a marginal improvement … without making an effective dent in the vast socio-economic disparities** in India.
- Chapter's verdict: **an effective instrument for mitigating the rigours of capitalism** which **fails to transform capitalism precisely for want of adequate, organized and consistent public pressure**.
- Directional criterion for the whole family: socialism **seeks to tilt the balance in favour of the weaker, exploited, underprivileged and oppressed sections**.
- ⚠️ Scandinavian and Indian illustrations and the market-socialism country list are **2009** observations.

## 7. What fascism is

- Origin: Italy, from *fascio*/*fasci*; early *fasci* were of workers; the movement demanded **revolutionary changes** in Italy's social and political structure.
- Strategy explanation of incoherence: principles embraced **only to win the political support of some groups**, especially **frustrated elements**; **since these heterogeneous groups had no common interest, no common ideal and no common values, fascism could never evolve a consistent political theory**.
- Mussolini (1924): the Fascists were **aristocrats and democrats, revolutionaries and reactionaries, proletarians and anti-proletarians, pacifists and antipacifists**, with **a single fixed point: the nation**.
- Laski: **an ill-assorted rag-bag**.
- Study rationale: read fascism **not for guidance in sorting out theoretical issues, but for understanding 'political pathology'** — how leaders mobilize people for **goals which they would not approve of under normal mental and social conditions**.

## 8. The spread of fascism

- Four degrees of attribution, kept separate by the chapter: **(1)** it **only developed into a movement which achieved temporary success in Italy**; **(2)** it was **partly adopted by Adolf Hitler (1889-1945) and the Nazis in Germany, Franco (1892-1975) and the Falangists in Spain**; **(3)** it **had a marginal following in Britain, France and other European countries**; **(4)** **some writers try to find its parallels in Asia, particularly in Japan, and in some Latin American countries, particularly in Argentina**. ❌ No other country or ruler is named.
- ⚠️ Unreconciled within the chapter: Ebenstein, quoted later, asserts that **Japan became fascist in the 1930s**, which is stronger than the chapter's own hedge.
- ⚠️ The closing remark that fascist tendencies persist is a **2009** observation; no contemporary state or movement is named as fascist in this session.

## 9. Fascism as a theory of reaction

- It **rejects equality** and **substitutes the principle of hierarchy culminating in a supreme leader or dictator whose will is law**.
- **Instead of regarding individual as an end and the state as a means, fascism exalts the state as an end and reduces individual to the means** — the inversion of the liberal relation.
- Liberty is redefined — in the Dunner definition the chapter quotes, **all values derive from the state, against which the individual possesses no rights**, and **true liberty, consequently, is found only in subjection to state authority**; the fascist state requires **complete conformity, rigid discipline, and unquestioning obedience**.
- Fascism **repudiates the progressive doctrine of human equality** and instead **seeks unity through homogeneity**; **in Germany this attitude was embodied in racist doctrines and the programme of liquidation of 'non-Aryan' elements**.
- It **does not tolerate any human association or organization within the state to compete with the state for the loyalty of individual**, thereby rejecting **the pluralistic cult of liberal-democracy**; internationally it **does not support international organization for settlement of disputes, and relies upon military solutions**.

## 10. Fascism as a theory of counter-revolution

- Two limbs, both required: fascism **sought to promote concentration of economic control in fewer hands as also to stop diffusion of political power**; **in other words, it stripped capitalism of its democratic character which could have had some sobering effect on it**.
- Consequences named: **lower wages**, **lowering of taxation upon capital**, **contraction of social services**.
- **Laski**'s functional claim: fascism amounted to **an insistence upon compulsory obedience to a state whose purpose is to protect existing class-relations**; the chapter also notes fascism was **strongly opposed to democracy (if not to capitalism)**.
- **Ebenstein**'s causal counter-claim: **the conditions of capitalism do not by themselves give rise to fascism, but it arises only where democracy is particularly weak**, because **Industrialists are not, as a class, any more fascist-minded than other social groups**, even though **it took only a few wealthy industrialists and landowners to supply fascist movements with ample funds**.
- Institutional evidence the chapter gives: the fascist states **brought the press, the wireless, the publishing trade, the cinema and the theatre directly under government control**, **the neutrality of the civil service was openly abandoned**, **even the judiciary was subordinated to the service of fascist ideals**, and **the whole state apparatus was used to advance the interests of capitalism at the expense of the worker**. ❌ It makes no claim about whether the leaders were capital's instruments.
- ⚠️ Functional and causal claims are compatible; the dispute is about origin, not about effect.

## 11. Myth and the conception of history

- Fascism **eulogizes the creative power of the myth to muster the support of all classes in society**; Mussolini: **we have created our myth … our myth is the nation, our myth is the greatness of the nation**; Hitler **similarly created the myth of race** and dwelt on **the miraculous power of propaganda, especially of false propaganda**, in **work *Mein Kampf* ('My Struggle') (1925-26)**.
- ⚠️ Lesson 17 records separately that Sorel **recommended the use of 'myth' to mobilize masses into action**. ❌ The chapter prints the two uses without connecting them; teach the **convergence**, do not assert a borrowing.
- Mussolini at Naples, 1922: **it is not necessary that it shall be a reality** — the truth-criterion is abandoned, which is what makes a political philosophy impossible.
- Against the economic conception of history, Mussolini's *Encyclopaedia Italiana* (1932) asserts that fascism **believes in holiness and in heroism; that is to say, in actions influenced by no economic motive, direct or indirect**, and that if **the economic conception of history be denied** then **an unchangeable and unchanging class-war is also denied**. ⚠️ It rejects materialist explanation without supplying an alternative analysis.

## 12. Socio-economic basis, criticism and verdict

- The chapter rejects the lower-middle-class thesis as a complete explanation (support was cross-class), ⚠️ while also reporting **Trotsky**'s description of the base as **a desperate, rootless, middle class** — the two passages are not reconciled.
- The equation of fascist and communist totalitarianism is **not only unfair but misleading**: fascism suppresses **to secure the interests of a tiny class**; communism, **even when it uses coercion**, **seeks to distribute benefits**.
- **Almond and Powell** locate the difference in **distributive capability**. ⚠️ Their sentence extracts defectively in the local scan and is reproduced with an ellipsis.
- Verdict: fascism is **a sick mental and political attitude**, and **the capitalists and the communists joined together** to defeat it.

## 13. What anarchism is, and the authority ledger

- Definition: a **mode of thought** holding that society **can and should be organized without the coercive authority of the state**.
- One common aim — **abolition of the state**; two disagreements — **how the state should be abolished** and **what type of organization should be evolved to replace it**.
- Range: **from the defenders of extreme form of socialism to the champions of extreme form of individualism**.
- **Rejected**: **compulsory forms of authority, culminating in authority of the state**; and for most, **hierarchical** authority — **churches, armies, bosses of capitalist enterprises, and impersonal bureaucracies**.
- **Retained** by most: **the rational form of authority, particularly the authority of experts**; and **the moral authority of collective decisions taken in a genuinely democratic manner**.
- ⚠️ Principle behind the ledger: retained authority is bounded by **competence** or by **consent**; rejected authority is bounded by neither.
- **Political authority** in the philosophical-anarchist sense — **the art of persuading others when they do not agree** — is compatible with anarchism; coercive authority is not.

## 14. The anarchist schools

- ⚠️ The chapter's list is flat, not nested: **philosophical anarchism, socialist anarchism, revolutionary anarchism, anarcho-syndicalism, pacific anarchism, and libertarian anarchism**. The grouping used in Map 3 is an analytical reading of that list, not a replacement for it.
- **Philosophical anarchism** — it **rejects the idea of legitimate authority in the sense that no individual, whether state official or not, has the right to command the obedience of another**, on the premise that **individual autonomy, as conceived morally, requires individuals to act according to their own judgments**. **Godwin** was **the first thinker who argued unequivocally for a stateless society**, and his *Enquiry Concerning Political Justice* (1793) is **regarded to be the first systematic defence of anarchism**. ❌ The chapter supplies no account of reason, education or moral development as the replacement for coercion; do not attribute one to it. ⚠️ The work is called **His *Enquiry*** at one point and **his essay** at another in the chapter.
- ⚠️ **'Individualist anarchism'** is the chapter's **second name for philosophical anarchism**, given **because of its focus on individual** — not a separate school.
- Within the same school, **Max Stirner (1806-56), a German philosopher**, in *The Ego and his Own* (1845) **argued that every individual is the unique one who truly 'owns himself'; he recognizes no duties to others, and does what is right for himself, within the limit of his might**. ⚠️ Standard reference: *Der Einzige und sein Eigentum* (1844), usually *The Ego and Its Own*. **R.P. Wolff** (*In Defense of Anarchism*; 1970) endorses Godwin's line **in contemporary thought**.
- **Socialist anarchism** — it **regards social and economic equality as a necessary condition to secure maximum freedom of all** and holds that equality **is incompatible with capitalist private property and the state**, so **it therefore rejects both**. **Proudhon**, its **chief exponent**, postulated **'mutual aid'** as the method, whence **'mutualism'**: **power of the state and power of capital are coterminous**, so **the proletariat cannot emancipate itself by acquiring and using state power**; society should be **organized as a network of autonomous local communities and producer associations, linked by 'the federal principle'**; **each person might possess his means of production (tools, land, etc.) either singly or collectively, but should only be rewarded for his labour**, which **will eliminate the elements of profit and rent**; supplemented by **a mutual credit bank which would lend to producers at a minimal rate of interest, covering only its cost of administration**. ❌ The chapter prints no property-is-theft formula; do not supply one. **Kropotkin**: communes and voluntary co-operation; distribution **according to needs**; human beings **not corrupted by the state and law** would develop **bonds of instinctive solidarity**.
- **Bakunin**: revolutionary anarchism; **propaganda by the deed**, extending to **acts of assassination and terrorism**.
- **Sorel**: chief exponent of **anarcho-syndicalism**, which the chapter calls **another version of revolutionary anarchism**, turning trade unions into **revolutionary instruments of class struggle**; yet **since Sorel relied on organized groups to overthrow capitalist state, and provide for its alternative, he is not regarded a full-fledged anarchist**.
- **Pacific anarchism** — **Tolstoy**: the state **tried to fight evil with another evil**; moral refusal, not seizure. The chapter records that **Gandhi was inspired by** Tolstoy **in developing his philosophy of non-violence**.
- **Libertarian anarchism** — **Hayek**, **Nozick**: **the revival of laissez faire individualism**; they **stand for restricting the role of the state to minimum possible level** and **do not recommend to abolish the state altogether**. Nozick's minimal state **would approximate to anarchy**. ⚠️ The same two thinkers are filed under neo-liberalism in Topic 03.
- Appraisal: **too optimistic** about human nature — if nature were **so benign**, the ills **would not have arisen at all**; and **terribly inadequate** at the scale of **worldwide terrorism, crime and environmental pollution**, where authority is **inadequate** and a **global authority** is needed to **supplement** it. Concession: anarchism's **significance lies in devising suitable means to curb that power**.

## 15. Gandhism — politics, ethics and means

- Politics is not autonomous: it is subordinated to ethics, and the moral teachings of religion supply the standard.
- **Means and ends are the two sides of a coin. They cannot be separated.** — an inseparability claim, not a preference: **as the means so the end**, and **realization of the goal is in exact proportion to that of the means**.
- The state is **a soulless machine for coercion of individuals**; when non-violence is **adopted as a universal principle**, the state **would become redundant**.
- **Swaraj** is glossed by the chapter simply as **independence from the foreign rule**, and it is subordinated to the ethic rather than the other way round: **"For me, ahimsa comes before swaraj."** ❌ The chapter supplies no moral-self-rule sense and no village-upward constitutional scheme; neither is asserted from this page range.

## 16. Truth and non-violence

- Truth (satya) is the end; non-violence (ahimsa) is the means and the test.
- Ahimsa is positive: not merely **abstention from violence** but **love of all**; **it is non-violence only when we love those that hate us**.
- Non-violence is **the power of the strong**, not the refuge of the weak; it **results in the 'change of heart' of the mighty opponent and makes him bend**.
- Violence is extended to the economic sphere: **acquiring material things beyond one's immediate need is also a form of violence because thereby we deprive others of their share**.
- **Gandhi adopted the path of satyagraha (reliance on the force of truth) for achieving the goal of swaraj … because this path was as sacred as its destination**, and **satyagraha involved the practice of ahimsa (non-violence) which embodied the right course of action**. ❌ The chapter does not describe satyagraha in terms of self-suffering or of converting the opponent; those are standard glosses and are not asserted here.

## 17. Trusteeship, bread labour, sarvodaya

- **Trusteeship**: owners should regard themselves **not … as the sole proprietors of their possessions, but only as 'trustees' of a gift bestowed upon them by God for the service of humanity**.
- Mechanism: Gandhi **relied on the power of truth and non-violence (ahimsa) to accomplish 'change of heart'**. Incentive: trustees would win **wide public respect instead of the existing hatred**. Realism: abrupt transition was **not feasible**, so the existing system was **allowed to continue with necessary changes in the attitude of the owners**.
- Chapter's own objection: the change of heart is **a fascinating idea, but … hardly realizable in actual practice**.
- **Bread labour**: class is rooted in **mental outlook based on contempt for physical labour**; compulsory physical labour produces **dignity of labour** and an equality **transcending the prevailing division of labour**.
- Technology: simple technology has **the capacity of mass employment**; advanced technology **would create vast unemployment, particularly in a country like India, and would promote consumerism with all its ill effects**.
- **Sarvodaya**: **'uplift of all', 'rise of all' or 'awakening of all'**; because it believes in uplift of all it **does not envisage a conflict between the high and the low, between the rich and the poor**, while it **gives prominence to the uplift of the deprived and the underprivileged sections**. ⚠️ Chapter-of-record for the fuller treatment is the common-good chapter, cited separately.
- ⚠️ Equality in this chapter covers **gender** and **faith**; ❌ caste is outside its range.

## 18. Gandhism and Marxism

- **Agreements**: both were **deeply concerned with the plight of the down-trodden**, and both **stood for a classless and stateless society** — but **their differences were more pronounced than their similarities**.
- **Disagreements**: unit of analysis — moral individual versus class; engine — **change of heart** versus class struggle; means — non-violence as identity-claim versus revolution; technology — simple and employment-generating versus large-scale industrial; property — held and used as trust versus socialised; the state — withers through moral transformation versus through the abolition of classes.
- ⚠️ The disagreement is generative, not merely oppositional: each supplies the question the other must answer — Marxism asks Gandhism for a mechanism, Gandhism asks Marxism for a justification of coercive means.

## 19. 🔶 Conservatism (owner's embedded module)

- 🔶 Entire section is reconstructed from the subject owner's module; **no page anchor in this chapter's range supports it**.
- Core claim: inherited institutions store practical experience that no designer can replicate, so the **burden of proof** lies on those proposing redesign.
- **Burke**: *Reflections on the Revolution in France* (**1790**); the Bristol Speech (**1774**) on the representative's judgment; against **Paine**'s *Rights of Man* (**1791**).
- **Disraeli**: *Sybil* (**1845**); one-nation conservatism and social obligation.
- **Oakeshott**: "Rationalism in Politics" (**1947**; collected **1962**); against the politics of the blueprint.
- **Hayek**: *The Constitution of Liberty* (**1960**), with the postscript disowning the conservative label.
- Reform doctrine: change **in order to conserve** — piecemeal, reversible, tested against demonstrated failure rather than against theory.
- Comparison: Fabian gradualism has a blueprint; Burkean gradualism distrusts blueprints; Gandhian change is the most restrained in means and the most radical in ends.
- ❌ Not fascism: conservatism defends an existing order through prudential reform; fascism **substitutes the principle of hierarchy culminating in a supreme leader**.

## 20. Separating the five, and answer architecture

- **Three discriminating questions**: (1) does the doctrine seek equality, seek hierarchy, or decline a designed end-state? (2) what is the state for? (3) how does change happen?
- **Fast separations**: socialism and anarchism share the equality aim and differ on the state; anarchism and libertarianism share the direction and differ on abolition; Gandhism and anarchism share the destination and differ on method and programme; fascism differs from all on the aim; conservatism differs on whether there should be a designed aim.
- **Four standing checks** before writing: provenance (chapter range, cross-chapter, or outside), date (2009 for all illustrative claims), boundary (no Indian provisions, schemes, parties or caste material), and names (**Sidney Webb**, **Sydney Olivier**, **Georges Sorel** in one's own voice).
- **Architecture**: 10 marks — one distinction, two evidence units, a closing line. 15 marks — claim restated as testable, evidence both ways, a logical attack, a finding. 20 marks — criterion declared, both halves assessed, objection-reply chain, conceded residual, graded verdict.

## Page-anchor convention

- The local PDF's page numbering runs **ahead** of the printed page numbering, and the offset is **not constant across the book**.
- **This topic's chapter**: PDF pp.**55-83** = printed pp.**41-69** — offset **14**.
- **Cross-chapter sarvodaya anchor**: PDF pp.**489-490** = printed pp.**470-471** — offset **19**.
- Every citation in this file gives the **PDF** page with the printed page in parentheses. ⚠️ No universal offset should be inferred from either figure.

## Evidence typing in this file

| Marker | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Blockquote | Source wording reproduced as such, subject to the OCR and presentational normalisations catalogued below |
| **Bold** | Ordinary emphasis, key terms, and editorial labels. ⚠️ Bold is **not** a quotation marker and carries no claim of verbatim identity |
| ⚠️ | Inference, caution, unresolved tension, or a limit on what the evidence supports |
| ✅ | A claim the source states directly |
| ❌ | An explicit boundary — material this topic does not own |
| ➡️ | A routing pointer to another topic's owner |
| 🔶 | Content from the subject owner's module that carries **no** anchor in this chapter's page range |

⚠️ Phrases carried from the source inside ordinary prose are reproduced as closely as the extraction allows and are identified by context; where compression was necessary the sentence is written as this session's own prose rather than presented as the source's. No exhaustive identity is claimed for every bolded token.

## Open uncertainties

1. ⚠️ **Sidney/Sydney Webb.** The chapter prints **Sydney Webb**; the standard form is **Sidney Webb (1859-1947)**. The subject owners also use *Sydney*, so the divergence is from the standard reference rather than between the local sources. Standard form recommended in a candidate's own voice.
2. ⚠️ **Olivier's first name.** The chapter prints **Sidney Olivier** in the roster at PDF p.58 and **Sydney Olivier** at p.59. The standard form is **Sydney Olivier (1859-1943)** — so against Webb, the two first names are effectively swapped relative to the standard.
3. ⚠️ **George/Georges Sorel.** The chapter prints **George Sorel**; the standard form is **Georges Sorel (1847-1922)**, which the subject owner uses.
4. ⚠️ **Stirner's work.** The chapter gives *The Ego and his Own*, **1845**. The standard reference is *Der Einzige und sein Eigentum*, **1844**, commonly translated *The Ego and Its Own*. Both the title form and the date differ.
5. ⚠️ **Quintus Fabius's dates.** The chapter gives **275-203 B.C.**; standard references often give c.280 B.C. for the birth. The chapter's figures are reproduced as the chapter's.
6. ⚠️ **The First International.** The chapter describes it as founded by the London Working Men's Association in **1864**; the standard description is the International Workingmen's Association, founded in London in **1864**. The year agrees; the founding-body description does not match the standard form.
7. ⚠️ **Japan.** PDF p.64 says only that **some writers try to find its parallels** in Japan, while the Ebenstein quotation at p.65 asserts that **Japan became fascist in the 1930s**. The chapter does not reconcile the hedge with the quotation it carries.
8. ⚠️ **Trotsky and the class base.** The chapter rejects the lower-middle-class thesis at PDF pp.69-70, then reports Trotsky's description of the base as **a desperate, rootless, middle class** at p.72, without reconciling the two.
9. ⚠️ **Almond and Powell at PDF p.73.** The sentence contrasting communist with fascist totalitarianism extracts without its subject in the local scan. It is reproduced with an ellipsis and the argument is supported from the chapter's own surrounding prose; it is **not** reconstructed.
10. ⚠️ **The PDF p.83 comparison table.** It extracts as interleaved column fragments because the scan reads the three columns down the page rather than across the rows; the **nine** issue rows were reassembled into their printed form. ❌ No cell is truncated in the scan, and none has been bracketed, completed or supplied from elsewhere. The four in-cell presentational corrections are catalogued in the normalisations table.
11. ⚠️ **Godwin's work.** Referred to as **His *Enquiry*** at PDF p.73 and as **his essay** at p.74; the inconsistency is the chapter's.
12. ⚠️ **Libertarian placement.** The chapter files **Hayek** and **Nozick** under libertarian anarchism while stating they **do not recommend to abolish the state altogether** — which is the family's defining aim. Topic 03 files the same two under neo-liberalism using the same **revival of laissez faire individualism** phrase. The tension is the source's and is taught as such.
13. ⚠️ **Syndicalism inside the evolutionary branch.** The roster at PDF p.57 counts syndicalism among the varieties alongside the evolutionary ones, while the **general strike** sits awkwardly with the **constitutional and peaceful** criterion. The chapter does not address the fit.
14. ⚠️ **Edition and date discipline.** All substantive claims come from the **Fifth Edition, 2009**. The Scandinavian and Indian illustrations, the market-socialism country list, the interwar material and the closing observation on persisting fascist tendencies are **2009** statements and are not presented as current. No contemporary state, movement or party is characterised as fascist in this session.
15. ⚠️ **PYQ transcription spacing.** The Philosophy Optional stems are reproduced as the local ledgers carry them. A sibling topic in this subject established that the local 2018-2025 transcription sometimes closes up a printed space before a question mark; the 2018 stem here preserves that space. Where official spacing was not independently confirmed in this pass, the ledger wording stands and the uncertainty is recorded here.
16. ⚠️ **2025 and 2026 General Studies papers.** The search pass covering reporting of those papers found no question naming these doctrines as political-theory doctrines. That is a finding over reporting, not a line-by-line reading of the official texts.
17. ⚠️ **Conservatism's provenance.** Lesson 23 and register §19 are reconstructed from the subject owner's §22 and carry **no** anchor in PDF pp.55-83. Only the bibliographic facts stated there are asserted: *Reflections* (1790), the Bristol Speech (1774), *Rights of Man* (1791), *Sybil* (1845), "Rationalism in Politics" (1947; collected 1962), *The Constitution of Liberty* (1960). The owner's three Gauba cross-references were carried as the owner's and not re-extracted.
18. ⚠️ **No official or current anchor was used.** None was found that links to these doctrines without importing policy or regime claims this topic does not own, and none was manufactured.

## Subject boundary

Political Theory is supplementary conceptual support for this subject stack; it is **not** a named owner of any General Studies paper. This file therefore claims no General Studies previous-year question and constructs no synthetic route to one. The Philosophy Optional stems used above are a **separate, explicitly labelled application source**, curated one per year with the empty year declared.

The boundaries respected throughout: ➡️ the content of Marxism belongs to **Topic 04**; ➡️ liberalism, neo-liberalism and the Hayek-Nozick architecture belong to **Topic 03**; ➡️ corporatism as institutional design is routed by the owners to the **democracy** file; ➡️ caste in Gandhian and Ambedkarite thought belongs to the **Philosophy caste owner**; ➡️ the theory of rights and of political obligation belong to the **rights** and **individual-and-state** owners; ➡️ the chronology of the World Wars, of decolonisation and of the Indian national movement belongs to the **World History** and **Modern India** owners. ❌ No Indian constitutional provision, government scheme, political party or named public figure is asserted from this topic.
